.
Parts of Concrete DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
and Symbolic DNN
\mathcal{D}_{S}

5.5.
Correctness of Verification Procedure

We define a notion of
over-approximation
of a concrete DNN by a symbolic DNN, a concrete value by a symbolic value, etc. So, any property proved by our verification algorithm for a symbolic DNN also holds for any concrete DNN that is over-approximated by the symbolic DNN. This notion lets us establish the correctness of the
ProveSound
verification procedure.

5.5.1.
Over-Approximation

Fig.
11
shows parts of a concrete DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
and a symbolic DNN
\mathcal{D}_{S}
from Fig.
2
b. The neuron
prev
in
\mathcal{D}_{S}
over-approximates the neuron
n
in the concrete DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
if
\varphi
is satisfiable, where
\varphi\equiv(\mu_{p}^{l}=-1)\wedge(\mu_{p}^{u}=3)\wedge(\mu_{p}^{L}=4+5n_{1}+6n_{2})\wedge(\mu_{r}^{1}+\mu_{r}^{2}*\mu_{n_{1}}+\mu_{r}^{3}*\mu_{n_{2}}=3-2n_{1})
. Further, if
\mu_{p},\mu_{n_{1}},\mu_{n_{2}}
represent
n,n_{1},n_{2}
respectively, they must also be equal, i.e.,
\varphi_{1}\equiv\varphi\wedge(\mu_{p}=n)\wedge(\mu_{n_{1}}=n_{1})\wedge(\mu_{n_{2}}=n_{2})
must be satisfiable. Note that the neurons in
\mathcal{D}_{C}
are not assigned any values and are therefore symbolic themselves. So,
\varphi_{1}
must be satisfiable for all possible values of
n,n_{1},n_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}_{C}
. Further, the symbolic DNN has another component
\mathcal{C}
which imposes constraints on
\mu_{i}
. So, the formula must be satisfiable under the constraints
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
\varphi_{2}
must be true.

(2)

\varphi_{2}=\forall\{n,n_{1},n_{2}\}\cdot\exists\{\mu_{p},\mu_{n_{1}},\mu_{n_{2}},\mu_{p}^{l},\cdots\}\cdot\big(\varphi\wedge(\mu_{p}=n)\wedge(\mu_{n_{1}}=n_{1})\wedge(\mu_{n_{2}}=n_{2})\wedge\mathcal{C}\big)

In the symbolic DNN,
\mathcal{C}
contains (i) the constraints encoded by the property
\mathcal{P}
assumed on all the neurons in the symbolic DNN, and (ii) the edge relationship between
curr
and
prev
.

Definition 5.0.

A symbolic DNN
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
over-approximates a concrete DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
if
\forall Y\cdot\exists W\cdot(\mathcal{C}\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))
, where
Y
is the set of neurons and
ProveSound
symbolic variables in
\mathcal{D}_{C}
and
W
is the set of all SMT symbolic variables in
\mathcal{D}_{S}
.

Further, in Equation
2
, all the variables inside universal quantifier (
n,n_{1},n_{2}
) are set equal to variables in the existential quantifier
\mu_{p},\mu_{n_{1}},\mu_{n_{2}}
. So, the equation can be rewritten by simply replacing the variables within the universal quantifier with corresponding variables in the existential quantifier, and removing the corresponding equality constraints, i.e.,
\varphi_{3}=\varphi_{2}
, where
\varphi_{3}=\forall\{\mu_{p},\mu_{n_{1}},\mu_{n_{2}}\}\cdot\exists\{\mu_{p}^{l},\mu_{p}^{u},\mu_{p}^{L},\mu_{r}^{1},\mu_{r}^{2},\mu_{r}^{3}\}\cdot(\varphi\wedge\mathcal{C})
.

In our example in Fig.
11
,
Y=\{\mu_{p},\mu_{n_{1}},\mu_{n_{2}}
}, and
W
is the set of all the other symbolic variables used in
\mathcal{D}_{S}
. So, a symbolic DNN
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
over-approximates a concrete DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
if
\forall Y\cdot\exists W\cdot(\mathcal{C}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}(\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)))
. There are two types of symbolic variables in
W
—ones that represent constants during concrete execution and ones that represent polyhedral or symbolic expressions. So, we partition
W
into two sets,
X
and
Z
, where
X
contains the symbolic variables representing constants, while
Z
contains the other symbolic variables. So, we can then re-write
\varphi_{3}
as
\varphi_{4}=\forall Y\cdot\exists X\cdot\exists Z\cdot(\mathcal{C}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}(\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)))
. Note that in the example above,
X=\{\mu_{p}^{l},\mu_{p}^{u},\mu_{r}^{1},\mu_{r}^{2},\mu_{r}^{3}\},Z=\{\mu_{p}^{L}\}
. From Equation
2
, since
\mu_{p}^{l},\mu_{p}^{u},\mu_{r}^{1},\mu_{r}^{2},\mu_{r}^{3}
are independent of
n,n_{1},n_{2}
, we bring the set
X
out of the
\forall
quantifier. Generalizing this notion, we use the definition
Over-approx-DNN
in Fig.
12
. The over-approximation of a concrete DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
by a symbolic DNN
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
is represented as
\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}_{S}
. The definitions for a symbolic value over-approximating a concrete value and a symbolic store over-approximating a concrete store can be found in (Appendix H).

Using these definitions of over-approximation, we prove two important properties. First, if a symbolic DNN over-approximates the concrete DNN, then expanding the symbolic DNN maintains the over-approximation. Second, we show that given a
ProveSound
expression that type-checks, if one starts with a symbolic DNN
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
and a concrete DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
such that
\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}_{S}
, then the output of applying symbolic semantics on
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
over-approximated the output of applying operational semantics on
\mathcal{D}_{C}
. We prove this using bisimulation (rule
Bisimulation
in Fig.
12
), where we simultaneously apply the operational semantics to the concrete DNN and the symbolic semantics to the symbolic DNN
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
. The complete details can be found in Appendix I.

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 16.09991ptX=\mathsf{Constants}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 16.09991ptY=\mathsf{Neurons}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\cup\mathsf{SymbolicVars}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996ptZ=\mathsf{PolyExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\cup\mathsf{SymExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\cup\mathsf{Constraints}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\exists X\cdot\forall Y\cdot\exists Z\cdot\Big(\mathcal{C}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)\Big)}{\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}_{S}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={{Over-approx-DNN}}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu\hskip 16.09991pt\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\exists!X\cdot\forall Y\cdot\exists!Z\cdot\Big(CS(\mu,\nu)\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\wedge\mathcal{M}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)\Big)}{\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab={{Bisumation}}}\end{array}

Figure 12.
Definitions for Over-approximation and Bisimulation

5.5.2.
Soundness and Completeness

We show that if
ProveSound
concludes that the abstract transformers specified in the program are verified to maintain the user-defined property
\mathcal{P}
, then executing the program on any concrete DNN also maintains the property
\mathcal{P}
. We prove this by initially creating a symbolic DNN with only the neurons representing
curr
and
prev
and edges representing their corresponding DNN operation (
\eta
- for example
ReLU
). This over-approximates any part of an arbitrary concrete DNN (within the bounds of verification) which is the output of
\eta
. Next, the over-approximation is maintained during symbolic DNN expansion and executing symbolic semantics. Finally, the query is generated over symbolic values that overapproximate the corresponding concrete values. So, if the SMT solver concludes that the property
\mathcal{P}
is maintained over the symbolic DNN, then we can conclude that
\mathcal{P}
will also be maintained over all over-approximated concrete DNNs. Further, since the symbolic semantics are not exact only for
traverse
and
solver
constructs,
ProveSound
is complete, excluding these constructs.

Theorem 5.3 (Soundness).

For a well-typed program
\Pi
, if
ProveSound
verification procedure proves it maintains the property
\mathcal{P}
, then upon executing
\Pi
on all concrete DNNs within the bounds of verification, the property
\mathcal{P}
will be maintained at all neurons in the DNN.

Theorem 5.4 (Completeness).

If executing a well-typed program
\Pi
that does not use
traverse
and
solver
constructs on all concrete DNNs within the bounds of verification maintains the property
\mathcal{P}
for all neurons in the DNN, then it can be proved by the
ProveSound
verification procedure.

6.
Evaluation

We demonstrate that designing the formal semantics for
ConstraintFlow
and the verification procedure
ProveSound
enables users to design and verify new DNN certifiers. The new designs include—(i) variations to the existing certifiers, (ii) supporting new DNN operations within the existing abstract domains, and (iii) completely new abstract domains and transformers. In practice, the implementations of existing DNN certifiers
(
Cousot and Cousot, 1977
;
Singh et al., 2019b
;
Zhang et al., 2018
;
Singh et al., 2018a
;
Singh et al., 2018b
;
Wu et al., 2022
)
employ various techniques to adjust the scalability vs precision tradeoff. Incorporating such modifications to the original algorithms unintentionally alters their mathematical logic. However, the original pen-and-paper proofs do not ensure the correctness of the certifiers with these modifications. In §
6.1
, we demonstrate that these modified certifiers can be verified using
ProveSound
by specifying them in
ConstraintFlow
. In §
6.2
, we extend DNN certifiers to support new operations such as
Abs
,
HardSigmoid
, etc. by designing abstract transformers, which has not been addressed by any existing work
(
Singh et al., 2019b
)
. We also show the verification of their soundness using
ProveSound
. In §
6.3
, we design new abstract domains and their corresponding transformers in
ConstraintFlow
and verify their soundness using
ProveSound
.

Finally, in §
6.4
, we show that
ConstraintFlow
can specify and verify the above-mentioned diverse existing DNN certifiers, covering various abstract domains, transformers, and flow directions. We evaluated a diverse set of state-of-the-art DNN certifiers, including IBP
(
Cousot and Cousot, 1977
)
, DeepPoly
(
Singh et al., 2019b
)
, CROWN
(
Zhang et al., 2018
)
, DeepZ
(
Singh et al., 2018a
)
, RefineZono
(
Singh et al., 2018b
)
, Vegas
(
Wu et al., 2022
)
, and Hybrid Zonotope
(
Mirman et al., 2018
)
. For all our experiments, we demonstrate that our verification procedure,
ProveSound
, can automatically prove the soundness of the certifiers specified in
ConstraintFlow
or detect unsoundness. The benchmarks for testing the unsoundness detection using
ProveSound
were created by introducing random bugs programmatically in the DNN certifiers, following a methodology established in prior research
(
Bugariu et al., 2018
)
. The details are provided in Appendix K.1.

DNN Operations

We focus on the widely used DNN operations, including primitive operations like
ReLU
,
Max
,
Min
,
Add
,
Mult
, etc., and composite operations like
Affine
,
MaxPool
, etc. The primitive operations are the ones that take a small, fixed number of inputs, like the addition or multiplication of 2 neurons. Since these can be composed to define composite operations, such as Attention layers, the corresponding abstract transformers can also be composed accordingly. Although verifying transformers for primitive operations directly implies the soundness of arbitrary compositions, in some cases, transformers can be more precise if specified directly for composite operations. In such cases, we show the specification and verification for composite operations.

We focus on the abstract transformers where the verification problem is known to be decidable. Although it is possible to express transformers for activation functions like Sigmoid and Tanh in
ConstraintFlow
(Appendix K.2), their verification may become undecidable
(
Isac et al., 2023
)
. In the future,
ProveSound
verification can be extended to handle these transformers using
\delta
-complete decision procedures
(
Gao et al., 2012
)
. Currently, our verification queries fall under SMT of Nonlinear Real Arithmetic (NRA), decidable with a doubly exponential runtime in the worst case
(
Khanh and Ogawa, 2012
)
.

Verification Bounds

For verification of composite operations -
Affine
and
MaxPool
, the parameters,
n_{prev}
(maximum number of neurons in a layer) and
n_{sym}
(maximum length of a polyhedral or symbolic expression) are used during the graph expansion step and impact the verification times. For our experiments, we set
n_{sym}=n_{prev}
. Note that
n_{prev}
is an upper bound for the maximum number of neurons in a single layer, without restricting the total neuron count in the DNN. Therefore, the DNN can have an arbitrary number of layers, each with at most
n_{prev}
neurons, thereby, allowing for an arbitrary total number of neurons in the DNN. We set these parameters based on the sizes of layers within DNNs that existing certifiers currently handle
(
Müller et al., 2021
;
Singh et al., 2019b
;
Zhang et al., 2018
;
Lorenz et al., 2021
)
. For
MaxPool
,
MinPool
, and
AvgPool
, existing certifiers handle at most
10
neurons at a time, so we set
n_{prev}=n_{sym}=10
. The
Affine
layer includes DNN operations like convolution layers and fully-connected layers. In Table
3(b)
, we present the computation times for
Affine
with
n_{prev}=n_{sym}=2048
. In Fig.
16
, we show how the verification time scales with parameter values (
n_{prev}=n_{sym}
), ranging from
32
to
8192
, for
Affine
transformers. Note that
n_{prev}=8192
corresponds to over 64 million parameters per layer. Existing DNN certifiers
(
Müller et al., 2021
;
Singh et al., 2019b
;
Zhang et al., 2018
;
Lorenz et al., 2021
)
usually do not operate on larger sizes than this, but the verification time for larger sizes can be extrapolated from the graph for higher values.

Experimental setup

We implemented the automated verification procedure in Python and used Z3 SMT solver
(
de Moura and Bjørner, 2008
)
to verify the generated queries. All our experiments were run on a 2.50 GHz 16 core 11th Gen Intel i9-11900H CPU with a main memory of 64 GB.

6.1.
Verifying Modified DNN Certifiers

Implementations of DNN certifiers often include modifications to balance the scalability vs. precision tradeoff. It is crucial to ensure the soundness of the modified certifiers. Verifying them using pen-and-paper proofs can be complicated. In contrast,
ConstraintFlow
and
ProveSound
provide a way to specify and verify these certifiers respectively. For illustration purposes, we focus on the DeepPoly abstract domain and key DNN operations—
Affine
,
MaxPool
, and
ReLU
. However, the concepts introduced can be applied to other certifiers and DNN operations. We present two case studies: BALANCE Cert and REUSE Cert, and show the evaluation results in Table
2(a)
.

6.1.1.
BALANCE Cert (Balanced Efficiency and Precision Certifier)

We use the same abstract shape as the DeepPoly certifier and design transformers that balance precision and efficiency.

Affine.
The most expensive part of the DeepPoly certifier is the backsubstitution step in the
Affine
transformer. To improve efficiency, albeit with reduced precision, BALANCE Cert employs a custom stopping function within the
traverse
construct to stop the backsubstitution at an intermediate layer, specifically, two layers back rather than always proceeding to the input layer.

ReLU.
In the case of unstable neurons, there are two commonly used lower polyhedral bounds - 0 and
prev
. In BALANCE Cert, a heuristic determines which polyhedral lower bound to store based on
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}}[l]
and
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}}[u]
.

MaxPool.

For
MaxPool
, we use the new abstract transformer designed in
(
Singh et al., 2024
)
, which is more precise than DeepPoly. We compute a list of neurons whose concrete lower bound is greater than or equal to the concrete upper bounds of all other neurons in
prev
. If this list is non-empty, we set the polyhedral lower and upper bounds to the average of the neurons in this list. Otherwise, we use the same polyhedral bounds used in DeepPoly. The complete code can be found in Appendix K.4.

6.1.2.
REUSE Cert (Reused Bounds for Enhanced Efficiency)

In an existing implementation of DeepPoly
(
Singh et al., 2018c
)
, the certifier stores previously computed polyhedral bounds from earlier layers to reuse them instead of recalculating them for current layer bounds. This approach prioritizes efficiency while accepting a slight trade-off in precision. In
ConstraintFlow
, this can be easily specified by additionally storing the cached polyhedral bounds as separate members of the abstract shape
L_{c},U_{c}
. For the
Affine
abstract transformer, the users can first use the new polyhedral bounds. If the results are not sufficiently precise (based on a heuristic), then the computation falls back to the original computation using the
traverse
construct. This transformer significantly boosts efficiency by leveraging cached values of previous
Affine
layer backsubstitutions rather than computing them anew at each layer. The transformers for
ReLU
and
MaxPool
can be similarly defined for REUSE Cert. The complete code can be found in Appendix K.4.

6.2.
Abstract Transformers for New DNN Operations

As deep learning frameworks continually introduce new activations, the need for designing
sound
abstract transformers becomes increasingly critical. We demonstrate the effectiveness of
ConstraintFlow
syntax and formal semantics and
ProveSound
verification procedure in this context by specifying and verifying abstract transformers for novel DNN operations not currently supported by existing DNN certifiers. These new operations include
ReLU6
,
Abs
,
HardSigmoid
,
HardTanh
, and
HardSwish
. Detailed transformers for each operation can be found in Appendix K. Evaluation results across different DNN certifiers are presented in Table
2(b)
, demonstrating that most transformers for these operations can be verified (or disproved) within 1 second. For illustration,
we show the DeepPoly transformer for
HardSwish
(
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{HardSwish}}}(x)=x\cdot\min(1,\min(0,\frac{x+3}{6}))
).

6.3.
Designing New DNN Certifiers with New Abstract Domains

We show that
ProveSound
allows verifying the soundness of new DNN certifiers based on completely new abstract domains and transformers. Specifying them in
ConstraintFlow
is only possible due to the novel formalism including type system and semantics introduced in this work.

SymPoly DNN Certifier

Several state-of-the-art DNN certifiers, including DeepPoly, CROWN, etc., approximate the value of each neuron in the DNN by imposing polyhedral constraints over each of them. However, in the case of piecewise-linear activation functions, these certifiers rely on heuristics to choose appropriate polyhedral bounds from more than one possible choice. For instance, in the case of an unstable
ReLU
neuron, there are infinite possibilities for a potential lower polyhedral bound. We argue that in general, the lower polyhedral bound can be of the form
c\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}}
where c is any real coefficient s.t.
0\leq c\leq 1
. The two most commonly used lower bounds -
prev
and
0
are only two extreme cases of the general lower bound. Using the
ConstraintFlow
syntax and semantics, the users can directly specify the general transformer, i.e.,
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}[L]\leftarrow\frac{1+\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{sym}}}}{2}*\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}}
.
ProveSound
can be used to prove the soundness of this lower bound. In this way,
ProveSound
allows a user to verify the soundness of a space of abstract transformers, which can be leveraged to automatically synthesize the optimal transformer using a cost function encoding the precision of the transformer based on the DNN certification problem. Further, since each invocation of the
sym
construct outputs a new symbolic value, different values of the symbolic coefficient can be chosen for different neurons in the DNN. A slightly different version is explored in the DNN certifier
\alpha-
CROWN
(
Xu et al., 2020b
)
, where
\alpha
is a concrete but learnable coefficient, learned using gradient descent.

Based on this idea, the DNN certifier SymPoly can be found in Fig.
13(a)
. The abstract domain consists of two concrete bounds
l, u
and two polyhedral bounds with symbolic coefficients
symL, symU
. The abstract transformer for
ReLU
is specified in 3 cases - (i)
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}[u]<0
, (ii)
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}[l]>0
, and (iii)
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}[l]\leq 0\leq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}[u]
. In the more challenging third case, the lower polyhedral bound is set to
\frac{1+\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{sym}}}}{2}*\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}}
.
The abstract transformers can be similarly designed for activations such as
HardTanh
,
HardSigmoid
,
HardSwish
,
Abs
, etc. These can be found in Appendix K.4. Notably, we can verify the soundness of these transformers in runtimes similar to the DeepPoly certifier.

Code Sketches for new DNN certifiers. The complete codes can be found in Appendix K.4

PolyZ DNN Certifier

We show another new abstract domain - PolyZ - a reduced product of the popular DeepZ and DeepPoly domains using polyhedral and symbolic constraints. The abstract shape consists of 5 members - two concrete interval bounds,
l
and
u
of the type
Real
, two polyhedral bounds
L
and
U
of the type
PolyExp
, and a symbolic expression
Z
of the type
SymExp
. The shape constraints state that the neuron’s value satisfies the bounds
l
,
u
,
L
, and
U
and
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}\ \texttt{<>}\ Z
. We also define the abstract transformers for this new domain. The
Affine
transformer is shown in Fig.
13(b)
and the complete specification is in Appendix K.4. PolyZ is more precise than both DeepPoly and DeepZ, and we can verify its soundness using the
ProveSound
verification procedure.

6.4.
State-of-the-Art DNN Certifiers

The existing DNN certifiers evaluated in this section include IBP
(
Cousot and Cousot, 1977
)
(Interval Bound Propagation), DeepPoly
(
Singh et al., 2019b
)
(or CROWN
(
Zhang et al., 2018
)
), DeepZ
(
Singh et al., 2018a
)
, RefineZono
(
Singh et al., 2018b
)
, Vegas
(
Wu et al., 2022
)
, and Hybrid Zonotope
(
Mirman et al., 2018
)
. The abstract shapes of DeepPoly, CROWN, and Vegas include polyhedral expressions represented by the
PolyExp
datatype and use the
traverse
construct to compute the concrete bounds. DeepZ, RefineZono, and Hybrid Zonotope use symbolic expressions represented by
SymExp
in their abstract shapes. RefineZono uses
Ct
to encode constraints over the possible values of the neurons. RefineZono and Vegas use the
solver
construct to compute the concrete bounds. The users can define functions using the
Func
construct, promoting code reusability and facilitating a modular design. The
ConstraintFlow
codes for these DNN certifiers are presented in Appendix K.

2.568

Notably, with the formal syntax and the operational semantics,
ConstraintFlow
can handle various flow directions effectively. For instance, the Vegas certifier
(
Wu et al., 2022
)
, which employs both forward and backward flows, is easily expressed in
ConstraintFlow
. We provide the code for the Vegas certifier in Fig.
6.4
. The abstract shape and the transformer for the forward direction are the same as the DeepPoly analysis, while the transformer for the backward analysis replaces operations like
ReLU
with
rev_ReLU
. We can also verify its soundness using
ProveSound
(Tables
3(a)
,
3(b)
).

For primitive operations like
Max
,
Mult
, etc., there are two implicit inputs to the transformer definitions, namely the input neurons -
prev0
and
prev1
. DeepPoly transformers for
Max
and
Mult
are shown in Fig.
14
.
The primitive operations -
ReLU
,
Max
,
Min
,
Add
,
Mult
shown in Table
3(a)
can be verified in fractions of a second. In Table
3(b)
, we show the evaluation results for the composite operations. For
MaxPool
and
MinPool
, the DeepZ and RefineZono transformers are harder to verify because their queries are doubly quantified due to the
<>
operator in their specifications. IBP is the easiest to verify because the limited abstract shape does not allow it to be as precise as other transformers for
MaxPool
and
MinPool
. Also, for Vegas, the backward transformers for
MaxPool
,
MinPool
, and
AvgPool
are not available in existing works. Similarly, for the Hybrid Zonotope, the transformer for
Affine
is defined in terms of transformers for primitive operations. So, we skip these in Table
3(b)
. For
Affine
, DeepPoly is the hardest because it uses the
traverse
construct, which requires additional queries to check the validity of the invariant. Vegas takes the least time because of a relatively simpler verification query. Note that the verification times are not correlated to the runtimes of certifiers on concrete DNNs. In Appendix K.3, we provide the
ConstraintFlow
code for several of these certifiers. The complexity inherent in these certifiers and their implementations suggests that verifying them solely through pen-and-paper proofs or automated theorem provers is impractical.

7.
Related Work

DNN Certification.

The recent advancements in DNN certification techniques
(
Albarghouthi, 2021
)
have led to the organization of competitions to showcase DNN certification capabilities
(
Brix et al., 2023
)
, the creation of benchmark datasets
(
Dario Guidotti and Pulina, 2023
)
, the introduction of a DSL for specifying certification properties
(
Geng et al., 2023
;
Shriver et al., 2021
)
, and the development of a library for DNN certifiers
(
Li et al., 2023
;
Pham et al., 2020
)
. However, these platforms lack formal soundness guarantees and do not offer a systematic approach to designing new certifiers.

DSL for Abstract Interpretation.

Although
(
Singh et al., 2024
)
proposed a preliminary design for
ConstraintFlow
using a few examples, the absence of formal semantics hinders its use for designing and verifying new DNN certifiers. We equip
ConstraintFlow
with a BNF grammar, type-system, operational semantics, and symbolic semantics that enable users to specify existing DNN certifiers, design new ones, and verify their soundness using
ProveSound
.

Similarly,
(
Lim and Reps, 2013
)
designs TSL—a DSL for abstract interpreters for conventional programs. TSL allows users to specify the concrete semantics and the abstract domain and automatically produces an abstract interpreter based on these specifications. However, it does not provide any specialized datatypes needed to specify DNN certifiers easily. It also does not guarantee the soundness of the abstract interpreter. In contrast,
ProveSound
can verify the soundness of the certifier specification.

Symbolic Execution.

Similar to
ProveSound
DNN expansion step,
(
Khurshid et al., 2003
;
Visser et al., 2004
)
employ lazy initialization for symbolic execution of complex data structures like lists, trees, etc. The object fields are initialized with symbolic values only when accessed by the program. Unlike these works, which possess prior knowledge of the exact structure of the objects, DNN certifiers deal with arbitrary DAGs representing DNNs. The graph nodes (neurons) are intricate data structures with unknown graph topology. We believe that we are the first to create a symbolic DNN with sufficient generality to represent arbitrary graph topologies to verify the soundness of DNN certifiers.

Correctness of Symbolic Execution.

Some existing works prove the correctness of the symbolic execution w.r.t. the language semantics
(
Keuchel et al., 2022
)
. However, these methods do not establish correctness in cases where symbolic execution also represents symbolic variables used in concrete executions. On the other hand, we provide elaborate proofs establishing the correctness of
ProveSound
where we encode the symbolic variables within the program as SMT symbolic variables.

8.
Discussion and Future Work

We develop
ProveSound
, a novel bounded automated verification procedure to automatically verify the overapproximation-based soundness of abstract interpretation-based DNN certifiers. We also develop a formal syntax, type-system, operational semantics, and symbolic semantics for
ConstraintFlow
. For the first time, we can verify the soundness of DNN certifiers for arbitrary (but bounded) DAG topologies. Given the growing concerns about AI safety, we believe that
ProveSound
, coupled with
ConstraintFlow
, allows the development of new DNN certifiers without proving their soundness manually. This work allows the following future directions:

Multi-neuron specifications

-
ProveSound
can be extended to verify multi-neuron abstract shapes
(
Müller et al., 2022
)
by allowing their specification in
ConstraintFlow
.

Sequence of Operations

-
ProveSound
can also be extended to automatically verify a sequence of DNN operations, like
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Affine}}}+\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{ReLU}}}
. To do so, while generating the final query, we would execute the concrete semantics of the composition of
Affine
and
ReLU
.

Automating Abstract Interpretation

-
ProveSound
and
ConstraintFlow
facilitate the automated generation of abstract transformers
(
Reps and Thakur, 2016
;
Kalita et al., 2022
;
Thakur et al., 2015
)
by offering all the basic components - (i) a DSL for defining the search space of candidate transformers, (ii) the semantics of the DSL, and (iii) a procedure for verifying the soundness of each candidate. This can be explored in future research.

Verification Property

- Currently, the verification property is the over-approximation-based soundness of a DNN certifier. Nevertheless, given that all the necessary formalism for verification has been established, the property can be extended to encompass more intricate aspects, such as encoding precision of a DNN certifier w.r.t. a baseline.

9.
Data-Availability Statement

The artifact
(
Singh, 2025
)
consists of
ProveSound
implementation and the
ConstraintFlow
specifications of the DNN certifiers presented in Section
6
and Appendix K. The code, accompanied by the instructions to run it, can be found
here
.

Other than standard types such as
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}
, we provide datatypes that are specific to DNN certification including
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Sym}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}
. Further, the expression of type
\overline{t}
are lists with all the elements of type
t
.

We have the standard unary, binary, and ternary operators for expressions. The list operations include computing the sum of a list, finding the maximum or minimum element of a list, the average of a list, the length of a list, the dot product of two lists, and concatenating two lists. Further, the construct
compare
can be used to define the maximum of a list of neurons which takes in a user-defined function that compares two neurons. Other operations are
map
,
mapList
,
traverse
,
solver
. function call. These are explained in the main text of the paper.

The metadata of a neuron is configurable. For now, we provide weight, bias, layer number, and equations as the metadata. This can be extended to include other things that are a part of the DNN architecture. For example, we can add boolean metadata such as the ones indicating whether a neuron is a part of the input layer or a part of the output layer. The metadata
equations
is used to refer to a list of equations relating the neurons from the current layer to the neurons of the next layer. When traversing the DNN in the backward direction, Each neuron in
prev
corresponds to one equation in
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}[\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{equations}}}]
. The metadata weight and bias are referred to as w and b in the main text.

Appendix B
Type checking

B.1.
Type checking Rules for Expressions

The general form for type-checking for expressions is
\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e\ :\ t
.
\Gamma
is a static record that stores the types of identifiers defined in the code. These identifiers include function names, transformer names, and the formal arguments of functions and transformers.
\tau_{s}
stores the types of the shape members defined by the user. The binary operators like
+,and,\leq
are overloaded to be also used for the new datatypes defined in
ConstraintFlow
. As in the rule
T-comparison-1
, the comparison of integers or real numbers outputs a boolean, however, in rule
T-comparison-2
, the comparison of two
PolyExp
expressions outputs a constraint of the type
Ct
.

\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e\ :\ t

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash x:\Gamma(x)}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-var}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash{\epsilon}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Sym}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-noise}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\sim e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-unary}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1},t_{2}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\}\hskip 8.19447pt\oplus\in\{\geq,\leq,==\}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-comparison-1}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\sqcup t_{2}=t\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\sqsubset t\sqsubset\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\oplus\in\{\geq,\leq,==\}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-comparison-2}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{2}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}\ \texttt{<>}\ e_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-comparison-3}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\oplus\in\{\mathsf{and},\mathsf{or}\}\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1},t_{2}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}\}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:t_{1}\sqcup t_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-binary-bool}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\sqcup t_{2}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}\}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\oplus\in\{+,-\}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:t_{1}\sqcup t_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-binary-arith-1}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{2}\sqsubseteq t_{1}\hskip 8.19447ptt_{2}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\oplus\in\{*,/\}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:t_{1}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-binary-arith-2}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Sym}}}\hskip 8.19447ptt_{2}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}*e_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-binary-arith-3}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\}\hskip 8.19447ptt_{2}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Sym}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\oplus\in\{*,/\}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-binary-arith-4}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\sqcup t_{2}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}\}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\sqsubset t_{2}\ \mathsf{or}\ t_{2}\sqsubset t_{1}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}*e_{2}:t_{1}\sqcup t_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-binary-mult}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{3}:t_{2}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash(e_{1}\ ?\ e_{2}:e_{3}):t_{1}\sqcup t_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-ternary}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e[\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{bias}}}]:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-metadata-b}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s}(x)=t}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e[x]:t}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-shape}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e[\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{bias}}}]:\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-metadata-b2}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s}(x)=t}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e[x]:\overline{t}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-metadata-shape2}}}\\
\\
\frac{F_{l}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{min}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}\}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\overline{t}\hskip 8.19447ptt\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash F_{l}(e):t}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-min-max}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\overline{t}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma(f_{c})=t\times t\rightarrow\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(e,f_{c}):\overline{t}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-compare}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma(f_{c})=(\Pi_{i}^{n}t_{i})\rightarrow t\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i,\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{i}:t_{i}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}):t}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-function-call}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\times\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\rightarrow t\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(f_{c}):\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-map-poly}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Sym}}}\times\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\rightarrow t\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(f_{c}):\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-map-sym}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\overline{t}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c}:t\rightarrow t^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\}\implies t^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Sym}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}\}\implies t^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\text{otherwise }t^{\prime}=t}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(f_{c}):t^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-map-list}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{solver}}}(\mathsf{op},e_{1},e_{2}):\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-solver}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c_{1}}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\rightarrow t^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c_{2}}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\rightarrow\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c_{3}}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\times\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\rightarrow t^{\prime\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\bot\sqsubset t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\bot\sqsubset t^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{1}.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e_{2}\}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-traverse}}}\end{array}

B.2.
Type checking Rules for Statements

The rules for type-checking statements update either
\tau_{s}
or
\Gamma
. For function definition statements where the arguments are of the type
t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}
and the return expression is of type
t
, the type of the function is
(\Pi_{i}^{n}t_{i})\rightarrow t
.
\Gamma
is updated to map the function name to this type. For transformer definition statements, if they take in the inputs
curr
and
prev
, which are optional, the inputs are of type
Neuron
and
\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}}
. The type of the output tuples returned by transformers are the join of the output tuples returned by the return expression of each operation. The type of each transformer is also stored in
\Gamma
. The type of the shape declaration statement is
[t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}]
, where
t_{i}
is the type of the
i
th declared shape member.

\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash s\ :\ \Gamma^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma[x_{1}\mapsto t_{1},\cdots x_{n}\mapsto t_{n}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptx\not\in\Gamma\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma^{\prime},\tau_{s}\vdash e:t\hskip 8.19447pt\bot\sqsubset t\sqsubset\top}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Func}}}\ x(t_{1}\ x_{1},\cdots t_{n}\ x_{n})=e:\Gamma[x\mapsto(\Pi_{i}^{n}t_{i})\rightarrow t]}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize{{T-func}}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma(\theta_{c})=\tau}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\theta_{c}:\tau}\quad\textsc{lab = {T-transformer-call}}\\
\\
\frac{\forall i\in[n],\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e_{i}:t_{i}\hskip 8.19447ptt_{i}\sqsubseteq\tau_{s}(i)}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}):(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-transformer-ret-1}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\theta_{r_{1}}:(t^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,t^{\prime}_{n})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\theta_{r_{2}}:(t^{\prime\prime}_{1},\cdots,t^{\prime\prime}_{n})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[n],t_{i}=t^{\prime}_{i}\sqcup t^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\top}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{if}}}(e,\theta_{r_{1}},\theta_{r_{2}}):(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-transformer-ret-2}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma[\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}\mapsto\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}})][\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}}\mapsto\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[m],\Gamma^{\prime},\tau_{s}\vdash\theta_{r_{i}}:(t^{1}_{i},\cdots,t^{n}_{i})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptx\not\in\Gamma\hskip 8.19447pt\forall j\in[n],t^{j}=\sqcup_{i\in[m]}(t^{j}_{i})\hskip 8.19447ptt^{j}\sqsubseteq\tau^{j}_{s}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Transformer}}}\ x(\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}})=\{\eta_{1}:\theta_{r_{1}},\cdots\}:\Gamma[x\mapsto(\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\times\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}})\rightarrow(t^{1},\cdots)]}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-transformer}}}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c_{1}}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\rightarrow t^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash f_{c_{2}}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\rightarrow\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\theta_{c}\in\Gamma\hskip 8.19447ptt^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Flow}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},\theta_{c}):\Gamma}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-flow}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash s_{1}:\Gamma^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma^{\prime},\tau_{s}\vdash s_{2}:\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}{\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash s_{1}\ ;\ s_{2}:\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-seq}}}\end{array}

\cdot\ \vdash d\ :\ \tau_{s}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\tau_{s}=[x_{1}\mapsto t_{1},\cdots x_{n}\mapsto t_{n}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[n],\bot\sqsubset t_{i}\sqsubset\top}{\cdot\ \vdash\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{{Def shape as}}}}\ (t_{1}\ x_{1},\cdots,t_{n}\ x_{n}):\tau_{s}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-shape}}}\end{array}

\cdot\ \vdash\Pi\ :\ \Gamma,\tau_{s}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\cdot\vdash d:\tau_{s}\hskip 8.19447pt\cdot,\tau_{s}\vdash s:\Gamma}{\cdot\ \vdash d\ ;\ s\ :\ \Gamma,\tau_{s}}\quad\textsc{lab={{T-program}}}\end{array}

Appendix C
Concrete Values in
ConstraintFlow

C.1.
Definition

\langle
\rangle

<constant>
c
::=
1
|
2
|
3
|
\cdots
|
\mathsf{true}
|
\mathsf{false}

\langle
\rangle

<PolyVal>
\nu_{p}
::=
c
|
c*n
|
\nu_{p}^{\prime}+\nu_{p}^{\prime\prime}

\langle
\rangle

<SymVal>
\nu_{s}
::=
c
|
c*{\epsilon}
|
\nu_{s}^{\prime}+\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}

\langle
\rangle

<CtVal>
\nu_{c}
::=
\nu_{p}==\nu_{p}
|
\nu_{p}\ \texttt{<>}\ \nu_{s}

\langle
\rangle

<Base-val>
\nu_{b}
::=
c
|
\nu_{p}
|
\nu_{s}

\langle
\rangle

<List-val>
\nu_{l}
::=
[\nu_{b_{1}},\nu_{b_{2}},\cdots]

\langle
\rangle

<Val>
\nu
::=
\nu_{b}
|
\nu_{l}

These are the possible values a concrete expression can evaluate using the operational semantics. The value can be a constant, integer, real number, or boolean. The value can also be a neuron or symbolic variable, or an affine combination of either, which would result in a polyhedral expression or symbolic expression. Since we allow solvers in the operational semantics, constraints, stating that two polyhedral values are equal or a polyhedral value is embedded in a symbolic expression, are also values. Lists of values are also valid values.

C.2.
Operations on Concrete Values

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\nu_{l})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\nu_{l}=[]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\nu_{l})=0}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-max-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu_{l}=[\nu_{b}]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\nu_{l})=\nu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-max-non-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu_{l}=\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}::\nu_{l}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\nu_{l}^{\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}\geq\nu_{b}^{\prime}\implies\nu_{b}=\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}<\nu_{b}^{\prime}\implies\nu_{b}=\nu_{b}^{\prime}}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\nu_{l})=\nu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-max-non-emp-r}}\end{array}

These are the rules for computing the maximum value of a list. The lists on which this operation is supported can only be of types
\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}}}
and
\overline{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}}
so the operations
\geq
and
<
are defined. These rules recursively compute the maximum element of the list by comparing each element to the maximum of the remaining elements in the list.

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{min}}}(\nu_{l})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\nu_{l}=[]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{min}}}(\nu_{l})=0}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-min-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu_{l}=[\nu_{b}]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{min}}}(\nu_{l})=\nu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-min-non-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu_{l}=\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}::\nu_{l}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{min}}}(\nu_{l}^{\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}\leq\nu_{b}^{\prime}\implies\nu_{b}=\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime\prime}>\nu_{b}^{\prime}\implies\nu_{b}=\nu_{b}^{\prime}}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{min}}}(\nu_{l})=\nu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-min-non-emp-r}}\end{array}

These rules are similar to the above rules, except they compute the minimum element of a list, instead of the maximum one.

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\nu_{l}=[\nu_{b_{1}},\cdots\nu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447ptF[f_{c}]=(x_{1},x_{2}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[n],\rho_{i}=\rho[x_{1}\mapsto\nu_{b},x_{2}\mapsto\nu_{b_{i}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\rho_{i},\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime}_{b_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime}=\bigwedge^{n}_{i=1}\nu^{\prime}_{b_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime}=\mathsf{true}\implies\nu_{l}^{\prime\prime}=\nu_{b}::\nu_{l}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime}=\mathsf{false}\implies\nu_{l}^{\prime\prime}=\nu_{l}^{\prime}}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{b},\nu_{l},\nu_{l}^{\prime},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\nu_{l}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Compare}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\nu_{l_{1}}=\nu_{b}::\nu_{l}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\ \nu_{l_{4}}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{b},\nu_{l_{2}},\nu_{l_{3}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{l_{5}}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l},\nu_{l_{2}},\nu_{l_{4}},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l_{1}},\nu_{l_{2}},\nu_{l_{3}},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\nu_{l_{5}}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Compare-non-emp}}\\
\\
\frac{\nu_{l_{1}}=[]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l_{1}},\nu_{l_{2}},\nu_{l_{3}},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\nu_{l_{3}}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Compare-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu_{l}=[]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\nu_{l}}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-compare-emp}}\\
\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\nu_{l}=[\nu_{b}]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\nu_{l}}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-compare-non-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu_{l}=[\nu_{b_{1}},\cdots\nu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{l}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l},\nu_{l},[],f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\nu_{l}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {V-compare-non-emp-r}}\end{array}

These rules define the
compare
operation. This operation takes as input a list and a function. The function has to take in two arguments, which will be members of the list, and return true if the first argument is greater than the second argument. The
compare
operation returns a list of all of the elements in the input list that are greater than all other elements. Depending on the function provided, this list could be empty, have one element, or more than one element. The rules
Compare
,
Compare-non-emp
, and
Compare-emp
compute the list of maximum elements while keeping track of an accumulated list of maximums. The rules
V-compare-emp
, and
V-compare-non-emp
state that the maximum of a list with 0 or 1 element is that same list.

C.3.
Value typing

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\vdash c_{i}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-int}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\vdash c_{r}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-real}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\vdash\mathsf{true}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-true}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\vdash\mathsf{false}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Bool}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-false}}}\\
\\
\frac{}{\vdash{\epsilon}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Sym}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-sym}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\vdash n:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-neuron}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\vdash c+\sum c*n:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-polyexp}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\vdash c+\sum c*{\epsilon}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-symexp}}}\\
\\
\frac{\vdash\nu_{1}:t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\vdash\nu_{2}:t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptt_{1}\sqcup t_{2}\in\{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Neuron}}}\}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\oplus\in\{\geq,\leq,==\}}{\vdash\nu_{1}\oplus\nu_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-ct-1}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\vdash\nu_{1}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\vdash\nu_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}}{\vdash\nu_{1}\texttt{<>}\nu_{2}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Ct}}}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-ct-2}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\vdash\nu_{i}:t_{i}\hskip 8.19447ptt=\bigsqcup^{n}_{i=1}t_{i}}{\vdash[\nu_{1},\cdots\nu_{n}]:\overline{t}}\quad\textsc{lab={{V-list}}}\end{array}

These are the types of possible values in concrete execution. Integers, real numbers, and boolean values have the standard types. Neurons, polyhedral expressions, symbolic variables, and symbolic expressions have the types
Neuron
,
PolyExp
,
Sym
and
SymExp
respectively. Constraints also use the specialized type,
Ct
. A list of values, which have the type
t
, has the type
\overline{t}

Appendix D
Operational Semantics of
ConstraintFlow

D.1.
Operational semantics for expressions

The general form for operational semantics for expressions is
\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu
.
F
is a static record that stores the function names, their formal arguments, and the return expression from the function definitions.
\rho
maps variables to values.
\mathcal{D}_{C}
represents the concrete DNN. It maps each neuron’s shape members and metadata to the corresponding concrete value, which comes from the input to the DNN certifier algorithm. The rules
Op-shape
and
Op-metadata
evaluate the expression before the shape member or metadata access, and then for the neuron, or each neuron in a list, output the value of the specified shape member or metadata from
\mathcal{D}_{C}
. The rule
Op-compare
refers to
Compare
,
Compare-non-emp
, and
Compare-emp
defined above. The
Traverse
rule maintains an active set, that starts from the neurons in its input polyhedral expression. Then, it filters out the elements of the active set for which the stopping condition,
f_{c_{2}}
, evaluates to true, using the rule
Filter
. After this, it applies the replacement function,
f_{c_{3}}
, to the neurons, and their corresponding coefficient, in the active set with the highest priority (found using the rule
Priority
). For each neuron to which the replacement function is applied, this neuron is removed from the active set and replaced by its neighbors (found using the rule
Neighbour
). The neighbors are defined as the nodes with outgoing edges to a neuron when traversing in the backward direction and the neurons with incoming edges from a neuron when traversing in the forward direction. All of these steps are repeated while the active set is not empty.

\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\langle c,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow c}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-const}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\langle x,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\rho(x)}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-var}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\langle{\epsilon},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow{\epsilon}_{new}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-sym}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}{\langle\sim e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\ \sim\nu}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-unary}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{2}}{\langle e_{1}\oplus e_{2},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{1}\oplus\nu_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-binary}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{b_{1}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{3},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{3}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b_{1}}=\mathsf{true}\implies\nu=\nu_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b_{1}}=\mathsf{false}\implies\nu=\nu_{3}}{\langle(e_{1}?e_{2}:e_{3}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-ternary}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow n\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu=\mathcal{D}_{C}[n[m]]}{\langle e[m],F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-metadata}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e.F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow n\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu=\mathcal{D}_{C}[n[x]]}{\langle e[x],F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-shape}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime}}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(e),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\max(\nu^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-max}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu^{\prime},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(e,f_{c}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-compare}}\\
\\
\\
\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\forall i\in[n],\ \langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptF(f_{c})=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\rho^{\prime}=\rho[x_{1}\mapsto\nu_{1},\cdots x_{n}\mapsto\nu_{n}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\rho^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}{\langle f_{c}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-func-call}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{b}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}^{\prime}=c_{0}+\sum_{i=0}^{i=l}c_{i}\cdot v_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[l],\ \langle f_{c}(v_{i},c_{i}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\nu_{b}=c_{0}+\sum_{i=0}^{i=l}\nu_{i}}{\langle e.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(f_{c}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-map}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}=\{v^{\prime}\ |\ (v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{V})\wedge(\langle f_{c}(v^{\prime}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\mathsf{false})\}}{\mathsf{Ft}(\mathsf{V},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Filter}}\\
\\
\frac{\forall v_{i}\in\mathsf{V},\langle f_{c}(v_{i}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptm=\max_{i}(\nu_{i})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{V}^{\prime}=\{v^{\prime}\ |\ (v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{V})\wedge(\langle f_{c}(v^{\prime}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow m\}}{\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{V},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})=\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Priority}}\\
\\
\frac{\delta=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{forward}}}\implies\mathsf{V}^{\prime}=\{v^{\prime}\ |\ (v,v^{\prime}\in E)\}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\delta=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{backward}}}\implies\mathsf{V}^{\prime}=\{v^{\prime}\ |\ (v^{\prime},v\in E)\}}{\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V},\delta)=\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Neighbour}}\\
\\
\frac{\nu=c_{0}+c_{1}\cdot v_{1}+\cdots+c_{l}\cdot v_{l}}{\mathsf{neurons}(\nu)=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{l}\}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Vertices}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu=c_{0}+c_{1}\cdot v_{1}+\cdots+c_{l}\cdot v_{l}}{\nu_{\mathsf{V}}=\sum_{v_{j}\in\mathsf{V}}c_{j}\cdot v_{j}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Vertices-2}}\\
\\
\frac{}{\langle\nu.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta^{\prime},f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C},\{\}\rangle\Downarrow\nu}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-traverse-1}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}=\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{V},f_{c_{1}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\nu=c+\nu_{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}+\nu_{\overline{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\nu_{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(f_{c_{3}}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu^{\prime\prime}=c+\nu^{\prime}+\nu_{\overline{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{V}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{Ft}((\mathsf{V}\setminus\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},\delta),f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\nu^{\prime\prime}.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathsf{V}^{\prime\prime}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\langle\nu.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathsf{V}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-traverse-2}}\\

\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Ft}(\mathsf{neurons}(\nu),f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\nu.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathsf{V}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime}}{\langle e_{1}.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{\_\},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-traverse}}\end{array}

D.2.
Operational semantics for
ConstraintFlow
statements

In operational semantics for statements, function definition statements modify
F
by adding the function name, arguments, and return expression. Transformer definitions similarly modify
\Theta
by adding the return tuples for each operation to
\Theta
, which is mapped to by the transformer name. The rules for the flow statement specify that if the constraints flow in the forward direction, the initial active set of neurons is the ones in the input layer. Then, this set is filtered to only retain the neurons for which the stopping condition is evaluated to
\mathsf{false}
. After this, the transformer specified in the flow statement is applied to each neuron in the active set. Lastly, each neuron in the active set is replaced with its neighbors. These steps are repeated until the active set of neurons in empty.

\langle s,F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow F^{\prime},\Theta^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Func}}}\ x(t_{1}\ x_{1},t_{2}\ x_{2},\cdots)=e,F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow F[x\mapsto((x_{1},x_{2},\cdots),e)],\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-func-def}}\\
\\
\frac{}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Transformer}}}\ x=\{\eta_{1}\rightarrow\theta_{r_{1}};\cdots\},F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow F,\Theta[x\mapsto(\{\eta_{1}\rightarrow\theta_{r_{1}};\cdots\})],\mathcal{D}_{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-transformer-def}}\\
\\
\frac{\forall i\in[n],\langle e_{i},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{i}}{\langle(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow(\nu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{n})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-transformer-ret}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\theta_{r_{1}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\theta_{r_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow t_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\nu=\mathsf{true}\implies t=t_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\nu=\mathsf{false}\implies t=t_{2}}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{if}}}(e,\theta_{r_{1}},\theta_{r_{2}}),F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow t}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-transformer-ret-if}}\\
\\
\frac{\Theta(\theta_{c})=\{\eta_{1}\rightarrow\theta_{r_{1}},\cdots,\eta_{m}\rightarrow\theta_{r_{m}}\}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall v_{i}\in\mathsf{V},\ \rho_{i}=[\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{curr}}}\mapsto v_{i}][\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{prev}}}\mapsto\mathsf{N}(v_{i},\delta)]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptv_{i}.\mathsf{type}=\eta_{j}\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\theta_{r_{j}},F,\rho_{i},\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow(\nu^{1}_{i},\cdots,\nu^{n}_{i})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{C}=\mathcal{D}_{C}[v_{i}.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{shape}}}\mapsto(\nu^{1}_{i},\cdots,\nu^{n}_{i})]}{\langle\theta_{c},F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathsf{V},\delta\rangle\Downarrow\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Transformer-call}}\\
\\
\frac{}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Flow}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},\theta_{c}),F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C},\{\}\rangle\Downarrow\mathcal{D}_{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Flow-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}=\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{V},f_{c_{1}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\theta_{c},F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathsf{V}^{\prime},\delta\rangle\Downarrow\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{V}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{Ft}((\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},\delta),f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Flow}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},\theta_{c}),F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime},\mathsf{V}^{\prime\prime}\rangle\Downarrow\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Flow}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},\theta_{c}),F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathsf{V}\rangle\Downarrow\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Flow-non-emp}}\\
\\
\frac{\delta=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{forward}}}\implies\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Ft}(\{v\ |\ v.\mathsf{input}=\mathsf{true}\},f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\delta=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{backward}}}\implies\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Ft}(\{v\ |\ v.\mathsf{output}=\mathsf{true}\},f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Flow}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},\theta_{c}),F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathsf{V}\rangle\Downarrow\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime}}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{Flow}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},\theta_{c}),F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-flow}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle s_{1};s_{2},F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow F^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle s_{1};s_{2},F^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime}\rangle\Downarrow F^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\langle s_{1};s_{2},F,\Theta,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow F^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Op-seq}}\end{array}

Appendix E
Type Soundness

E.1.
Type-checking for expressions

Lemma
4.1
.

If,

(1)

\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e:t

(2)

\bot\sqsubset t\sqsubset\top

(3)

F\sim\Gamma,\tau_{s}

(4)

\rho\sim\Gamma

(5)

\mathcal{D}_{C}\sim\tau_{s}

(6)

\mathcal{D}_{C}
is finite

then,

(1)

\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu

(2)

\vdash\nu:t^{\prime}

(3)

t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq t

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that if an expression type-checks, then it will terminate in the operational semantics and evaluate to a value. This value will either be the same type that the expression type checks to, or a subtype. We prove this lemma for all expressions, using induction on the structure of an expression.
To prove the termination of every expression in
ConstraintFlow
, the only non-trivial case is
traverse
. For this case, we use a ranking function and show that it is bounded by 0 and decreases by at least 1 in each iteration.
∎

Proof.

Proof by induction on the structure of
e

Base Cases:

\mathbf{e\equiv c}

\displaystyle e\equiv c_{i}\wedge\Gamma,\tau_{s}\models e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}}\text{ or }e\equiv c_{f}\wedge\Gamma,\tau_{s}\models e:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}

From
T-const

\displaystyle...(1)

\displaystyle\text{From {Op-const}}\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow c

\displaystyle...(2)

\displaystyle\text{From {V-int} and {V-real} }\vdash c_{i}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}}\text{ and }\vdash c_{f}:\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}

Consequent 2

\displaystyle...(3)

\displaystyle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Int}}}\text{ and }\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{Real}}}

Consequent (3)

\displaystyle...(5)

From
Op-traverse, Op-traverse-1, Op-traverse-2
,

Neighbour, Filter, Priority, Vertices and Vertices-2

The following are the steps to the traverse operational semantics:

\displaystyle...(6)

a.

Start with an active set of neurons,
\mathsf{neurons}(\nu_{2})
equals to the neurons in the polyhedral expression
\nu_{2}
.

b.

Create
\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{Ft}(\mathsf{neurons}(\nu_{2}),f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})

c.

If
\mathsf{V}=\emptyset
, output
\nu_{2}
. Otherwise, do steps d through g below.

d.

Create
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}=\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{V},f_{c_{1}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})

e.

\nu_{2}=c+\nu_{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}+\nu_{\overline{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}}

\nu^{\prime}_{2}=c+\nu_{\overline{\mathsf{V}^{\prime}}}+\sum_{n}f_{c_{3}}(c_{n},n)

where
n
is all the neurons in
\nu_{2}
that are also in
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}
and
c_{n}
is the coefficient of
n
in
\nu_{2}
.

f.

Create
\mathsf{V}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{Ft}((\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},\delta),f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})

g.

\mathsf{V}=\mathsf{V}^{\prime\prime}\quad\nu_{2}=\nu^{\prime}_{2}
Go back to step c.

To prove that this algorithm terminates we will define
L(V)
as follows. Since the DNN has to be a DAG, there is a topological ordering of the neurons in the DNN,
n_{0},n_{1},\cdots,n_{k}
. Let’s assume, without loss of generality, that we are traversing the DNN in the backward direction. We will define a function

\ell_{\mathsf{V}}(n)=\begin{cases}1,n\in\mathsf{V}\\
0,n\notin\mathsf{V}\\
\end{cases}

We will define
L(\mathsf{V})
to be the binary number represented by
\ell_{\mathsf{V}}(n_{k})\ell_{\mathsf{V}}(n_{k-1})\cdots\ell_{\mathsf{V}}(n_{1})\ell_{\mathsf{V}}(n_{0})

L(\mathsf{V})
is bounded by 0 by the definition of
\ell_{\mathsf{V}}
.

We will show that the algorithm above terminates by showing that
L(\mathsf{V})
decreases by at least 1 in each iteration of the loop.

(1)

P returns the subset of
\mathsf{V}
containing all of the neurons with the highest priority.
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{V}

(2)

From (1) and the definition of
L
,
L(\mathsf{V})=L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime})+L(\mathsf{V}\setminus\mathsf{V}^{\prime})

(3)

\mathsf{V}^{\prime}={n_{v_{1}},\cdots,n_{v_{j}}}
. We know
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}
is not empty because
\mathsf{V}
is not empty(otherwise the loop would be over), and since there are a finite number of neurons in the DNN, and therefore, a finite number in
\mathsf{V}
there has to be at least one neuron in
\mathsf{V}
with the highest priority. We can define
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathsf{V}^{\prime}
and for
i\in[j]
, we can define
\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i-1}\setminus n_{v_{i}}\cup\{n^{\prime}|(n^{\prime},n)\in E\}

(4)

When going in the backward direction,
L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i-1}\setminus n_{v_{i}})=L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i-1})-2^{p}
where
p
is the index of
n_{v_{i}}
on the topological order.
L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i-1}\setminus n_{v_{i}}\cup\{n^{\prime}|(n^{\prime},n)\in E\})<=L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i-1})-2^{p}+\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}2^{i}
because when traversing backwards, every neighbor has to be before
n_{v_{i}}
in the topological sort. Therefore,
L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i})<L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{i-1})
.

(5)

\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{backward}}})=\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{j}
From (4),
L(\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{backward}}}))=L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime}_{j})<L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime})

(6)

L((\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},d))<=L(\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})+L(\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},d))

(7)

From (5),
L(\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})+L(\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},d))<L(\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})+L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime})=L(\mathsf{V})

(8)

\mathsf{Ft}
returns a subset of the set of neurons passed to it.

Therefore,
L(\mathsf{Ft}((\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},d)),f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})<=L((\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},d))

(9)

From (6), (7) and (8),
L(\mathsf{Ft}((\mathsf{V}-\mathsf{V}^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{V}^{\prime},d)),f_{c_{2}},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})<L(\mathsf{V})

(10)

From (9),
L(\mathsf{V}^{\prime\prime})<L(\mathsf{V})
Therefore,
L(\mathsf{V})
decreases in each iteration of the loop. Since it is a binary number that is bounded by 0, this means the loop must terminate.

Now that we have shown that this algorithm terminates and produces a value,
\nu
, under operational semantics (Consequent (1)), we still have to prove that
\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\nu:t
.

\displaystyle\vdash\nu_{2}:t_{2}\quad t_{2}\sqsubseteq\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}
