E.2.
Type-checking for statements

Lemma
4.2
.

If,

(1)

\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash s:\Gamma^{\prime}

(2)

F\sim\Gamma,\tau_{s}

(3)

\rho\sim\Gamma

(4)

\mathcal{D}_{C}\sim\tau_{s}

(5)

\mathcal{D}_{C}
is finite

then,

(1)

\langle s,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow F^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{C}

(2)

F^{\prime}\sim\Gamma^{\prime},\tau_{s}

(3)

\rho^{\prime}\sim\Gamma^{\prime}

(4)

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{C}\sim\tau_{s}

Proof.

For function declaration statements and transformer definition statements, the expressions within them are successfully type-checked. So, according to Lemma
4.1
, the updated
F
and
\Theta
remains consistent with
\Gamma^{\prime},\tau_{s}
after adding the new mapping.
For
Flow
statements, an already defined abstract transformer is applied to the DNN
\mathcal{D}_{C}
. Since
\Theta
is consistent with
\Gamma,\tau_{s}
, after each application of the abstract transformer, the updated DNN
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{C}
remains consistent with
\Gamma^{\prime},\tau_{s}
.
∎

E.3.
Type soundness theorem

Theorem
4.3
.

A well-typed program in
ConstraintFlow
successfully terminates according to the operational semantics, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}\models\mathcal{OP}
. Formally, if
\cdot\vdash\Pi:\Gamma,\tau_{s}
then
\langle\Pi,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{C}

The proof follows from Lemmas
4.1
and
4.2
.

Appendix F
Symbolic Values for Verification Procedure

F.1.
Definition

\langle
\rangle

<Base-sym-val>
\mu_{b}
::=
\mu_{r}
|
\mu_{i}
|
\mu_{b}
|
c

|
\neg\mu_{1}
|
\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}

|
If
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})

\langle
\rangle

<List-sym-val>
\mu_{l}
::=
[\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{2}},\cdots]

|
If(\mu_{b},\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})

\langle
\rangle

<Sym-val>
\mu
::=
\mu_{b}
|
\mu_{l}

These are the possible values a symbolic expression can be evaluated to using symbolic semantics. A symbolic value can be a symbolic variable of type real, integer, or boolean, a unary or binary operation applied to a symbolic variable, and a conditional value of the form
if(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})
. Integer, boolean, or real constants can also be symbolic values because sometimes the exact constant is known even during symbolic execution. Lists of symbolic values and conditional operations on lists of symbolic values are also considered symbolic values.

F.2.
Operations on Symbolic Values

0pt(\mu_{l})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{2}},\cdots]}{0pt(\mu_{l})=0}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-height-b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{l}=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptc_{1}=0pt(\mu_{l_{1}})\hskip 8.19447ptc_{2}=0pt(\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptc=1+\max(c_{1},c_{2})}{0pt(\mu_{l})=c}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-height-r}}\end{array}

0pt(\mu_{l})
is used to determine the maximum number of nested conditional values in the branches of a conditional value.
0pt(\mu_{l})
is 0 when called on a symbolic value that is not conditional. This is used to prove lemmas that involve values of the form
[\mu_{b_{1}},\cdots,\mu_{b_{n}}]
, which can be proved by induction on
0pt(\mu_{l})
. A similar notion can be defined for basic values to find the number of nested conditional symbolic values before a basic symbolic value that is not conditional is reached.

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{b}=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum\mu_{b_{i}}*n_{i}}{\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{b})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expanded-poly}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{b}=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum\mu_{b_{i}}*\epsilon_{i}}{\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{b})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expanded-sym}}\\
\\
\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b_{0}},\cdots,\mu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[n],\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{b_{i}})}{\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{l})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expanded-list}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu=If(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{2})\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{3})}{\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expanded-if}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{1})\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{2})}{\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expanded-binary}}\end{array}

These rules define expanded values that are necessary when symbolically executing functions like
map
and
traverse
because these operations apply a function to each pair of coefficient and neuron, or coefficient and symbolic variable, in a polyhedral or symbolic expression. Here, a polyhedral expression or symbolic expression has to have each of its constituent neurons or symbolic variables represented explicitly. as opposed to the whole polyhedral or symbolic variable being defined by one symbolic variable of type real, which is how these expressions are initially defined.

\mathsf{sum}(\mu_{l})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{2}},\cdots\mu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\mu_{b_{i}}}{\mathsf{sum}(\mu_{l})=\mu_{b}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-sum-b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu^{\prime}_{b_{1}}=\mathsf{sum}(\mu_{l_{1}})\hskip 8.19447pt\mu^{\prime}_{b_{2}}=\mathsf{sum}(\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=If(\mu_{b},\mu^{\prime}_{b_{1}},\mu^{\prime}_{b_{2}})}{\mathsf{sum}(\mu_{l})=\mu_{b}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-sum-r}}\end{array}

These rules compute the sum of a list symbolic value. The sum of a list is the sum of each element in the list. The sum of a conditional symbolic value,
if(\mu_{b},\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})
is either the sum of
\mu_{l_{1}}
or the sum of
\mu_{l_{2}}
depending on the condition
\mu_{b}
.

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{l_{1}}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{2}},\cdots\mu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l_{2}}=[\mu^{\prime}_{b_{1}},\mu^{\prime}_{b_{2}},\cdots\mu^{\prime}_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=(\sum^{\min(n,m)}_{i=1}\mu_{b_{i}}*\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})=\mu_{b}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-dot-b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{l_{1}}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{2}},\cdots\mu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l_{2}}=If(\mu_{b},\mu^{\prime}_{l_{2}},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu^{\prime}_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime\prime}=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})=\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-dot-r1}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{l_{1}}=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mu_{l}^{\prime\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l}^{\prime\prime},\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime\prime}=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{dot}}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})=\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-dot-r2}}\end{array}

These rules compute the dot product of two lists. Similar to
\mathsf{sum}
, this operation is defined recursively. However, since there are two lists, the recursion needs to be done on both.

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(\mu_{b},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{b}=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum_{i=0}^{l}\mu_{b_{i}}\cdot n_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.19447ptF(f_{c})=(x_{1},x_{2}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[l],\ \sigma_{i}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{b_{i}},x_{2}\mapsto n_{i}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma_{i},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum_{i=0}^{l}\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}}}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(\mu_{b},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{l}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-map-poly}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{b}=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum_{i=0}^{l}\mu_{b_{i}}\cdot\epsilon_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.19447ptF(f_{c})=(x_{1},x_{2}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[l],\ \sigma_{i}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{b_{i}},x_{2}\mapsto\epsilon_{i}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma_{i},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum_{i=0}^{l}\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}}}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(\mu_{b},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{l}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-map-sym}}\\
\\
\frac{\mu_{b}=If(\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{2}},\mu_{b_{3}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(\mu_{b_{2}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu^{\prime}_{b_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(\mu_{b_{3}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{2})=\mu^{\prime}_{b_{3}},\mathcal{C}_{3}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=If(\mu_{b_{1}},\mu^{\prime}_{b_{2}},\mu^{\prime}_{b_{3}})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(\mu_{b},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{3}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-map-r}}\end{array}

The
map
function applies
f_{c}
to each pair of neuron and coefficient in a polyhedral expression or symbolic variable and coefficient in a symbolic expression and then returns the sum of the outputs of each application of
f_{c}
. This has to be defined recursively because the top-level symbolic value might be a conditional symbolic value.

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{mapList}}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\cdots,\mu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptF(f_{c})=(x_{1}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[l],\ \sigma_{i}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{b_{i}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma_{i},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l}^{\prime}=[\mu^{\prime}_{b_{1}},\cdots,\mu^{\prime}_{b_{n}}]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{mapList}}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{n}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-maplist-b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{l}=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{mapList}}}(\mu_{l_{1}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu^{\prime}_{l_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{mapList}}}(\mu_{l_{2}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{1})=\mu^{\prime}_{l_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l}^{\prime}=If(\mu_{b},\mu^{\prime}_{l_{1}},\mu^{\prime}_{l_{2}})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{mapList}}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-maplist-r}}\end{array}

The
mapList
function applies
f_{c}
to each element in a list, and outputs a list of the outputs of each application of
f_{c}
. Similar to
map
above, this function also has to be defined recursively.

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{b}=If(\mu_{b}^{\prime}\geq\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime},\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime})=\mu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-max}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{b}=If(\mu_{b_{1}}\leq\mu_{b_{2}},\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{2}})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{min}}}(\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{b_{1}})=\mu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-min}}\end{array}

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{l}=[]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l})=0}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-max-emp}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b}]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l})=\mu_{b}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-max-b-non-emp-1}}\\
\\
\frac{\mu_{l}=\mu_{b}::\mu_{l}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l}^{\prime})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l})=If(\mu_{b}\geq\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b},\mu_{b}^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-max-b-non-emp-r}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{l}=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mu_{l}^{\prime\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l}^{\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l}^{\prime\prime})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu_{l})=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime})}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-max-r}}\end{array}

The functions
max
and
min
are overloaded since they are defined on two symbolic variables and defined on a list of symbolic variables. In the former case, they output a conditional value representing the maximum or minimum of the two input symbolic values. In the latter case, they output a conditional value representing the maximum or minimum element in the list of symbolic values. Again, for the list operation, the function must be defined recursively. Here, it is valid to compare elements with
\geq
and
\leq
because these operations are defined symbolically for the types of basic symbolic values stated above.

\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\cdots\mu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447ptF[f_{c}]=(x_{1},x_{2}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[n],\sigma_{i}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{b},x_{2}\mapsto\mu_{b_{i}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma_{i},\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\bigwedge^{n}_{i=1}\mu^{\prime}_{b_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l}^{\prime\prime}=If(\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mu_{b}::\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mu_{l}^{\prime})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{b},\mu_{l},\mu_{l}^{\prime},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}_{n}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-compare}}\\
\\
\frac{\mu_{l_{1}}=[]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}},\mu_{l_{3}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l_{3}},\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-compare-emp}}\frac{\mu_{l_{1}}=\mu_{b}::\mu_{l}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\ \mu_{l_{4}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{b},\nu_{l_{2}},\mu_{l_{3}},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l_{5}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},\nu_{l_{2}},\mu_{l_{4}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}},\mu_{l_{3}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l_{5}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-compare-non-emp}}\\
\\
\frac{\mu_{l}=[]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l},\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-compare-emp}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b}]}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l},\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-compare-non-emp}}\\
\\
\frac{\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\cdots\mu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},\mu_{l},[],f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-compare-non-emp-r}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mu_{l}=If(\mu_{b},\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu^{\prime}_{l_{1}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l_{1}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu^{\prime}_{l_{2}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l_{2}},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l}^{\prime}=If(\mu_{b},\mu^{\prime}_{l_{1}},\mu^{\prime}_{l_{2}})}{\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{l}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sval-compare-if}}\\
\\
\end{array}

These rules are similar to the rules for
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\nu_{l},f_{c},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C})
. The difference is that these rules are defined on symbolic values using symbolic semantics instead of concrete values and concrete semantics. The main change is that instead of computing the maximum element according to
f_{c}
, the output is a nested conditional symbolic value where every combination of maximum elements appears. For example, if the list were
[\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}]
, the output of
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu_{l},f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})
is
if(f_{c}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\land f_{c}(\mu_{1},\mu_{3}),\mu_{1}::(if(f_{c}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\land f_{c}(\mu_{1},\mu_{3}),\mu_{2}::\cdots,\cdots),\cdots)
.

Appendix G
Symbolic Semantics of
ConstraintFlow

G.1.
Symbolic DNN Expansion

\mathsf{expand}(e,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
outputs a new symbolic DNN, where the shape members/metadata that are of type
PolyExp
or
SymExp
that are represented by one variable are expanded. Expanding a
PolyExp
variable entails adding neurons to
\mathcal{D}_{S}
, adding the shape constraint applied to those neurons to
\mathcal{C}
, and declaring real-valued symbolic variables to represent the constants in the
PolyExp
. Expanding a
SymExp
variable entails declaring real-valued symbolic variables for the constants in the expanded symbolic expression and adding a constraint for each concrete symbolic variable.

\mathsf{expand}(e,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\vdash m:t^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447ptt=\mathcal{R}(t^{\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu=\mu^{t}_{new}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}_{S}[n[m]\mapsto\mu]}{\mathsf{add}(n,m,\mathcal{D}_{S})=\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Add-metadata-elem}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\tau_{s}(x)=t^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{R}(t^{\prime})=t\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu=\mu^{t}_{new}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}_{S}[n[x]\mapsto\mu]}{\mathsf{add}(n,x,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S})=\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Add-shape-elem}}\\
\\
\frac{n\in\mathcal{D}_{S}}{\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Add-neuron-b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{n\not\in\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\textsf{Metadata}[m_{1},\cdots,m_{k}]\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[k],\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{add}(n,m,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S_{i-1}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\textsf{Shape}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{l}]\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S_{k}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[l],\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{add}(n,x_{i},\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{i-1}})}{\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{D}_{S_{l}}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}\wedge\mathcal{P}((\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{l}}(n[x_{1}]),\cdots),n)}\quad\textsc{lab = {Add-neuron-r}}\\
\\
\frac{n[x]=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum\mu_{b_{i}}*n_{i}}{\mathsf{expandN}(n,x,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathsf{true}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expand-poly-b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{n[x]=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum\mu_{b_{i}}*\epsilon_{i}}{\mathsf{expandN}(n,x,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathsf{true}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expand-sym-b}}\\
\\
\frac{\tau_{s}(x)=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{PolyExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S}{n[x]}=\mu_{b_{r}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{N}=[n^{\prime}_{1},\cdots n^{\prime}_{j}]\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j],\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathsf{add}(n^{\prime}_{i},\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S_{i-1}},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}_{i-1})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mu_{r_{0}}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}\mu_{r_{i}}*n^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}_{S_{j}}[n[x]\mapsto\mu_{b}]}{\mathsf{expandN}(n,x,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}_{0})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{j}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expand-poly-r}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\tau_{s}(x)=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{SymExp}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S}{n[x]}=\mu_{b_{r}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{E}=[\mu^{\prime}_{1},\cdots\mu^{\prime}_{j}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j],\mathcal{C}_{i}=(-1\leq\mu^{\prime}_{i}\leq 1)\wedge\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mu_{0}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}\mu_{i}*n^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}_{S_{j}}[n[x]\mapsto\mu_{b}]}{\mathsf{expandN}(n,x,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}_{0})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{j}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Expand-sym-r}}\\
\\
\frac{}{\mathsf{expand}(c,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {E-const}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mathsf{expand}(e_{1},\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{expand}(e_{2},\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathsf{expand}(e_{1}\oplus e_{2},\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {E-binary}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathsf{expand}(e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{s},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rangle\downarrow n,\_\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{expandN}(n,x,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathsf{expand}(e[x],\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {E-shape-b}}\frac{\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow[n_{1},\cdots n_{q}]\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[q],\mathsf{expandN}(n,x,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S_{i-1}},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}}{\mathsf{expand}(e[x],\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S_{q}},\bigwedge^{q}_{i=1}\mathcal{C}_{i}}\quad\textsc{lab = {E-shape-r}}\par\end{array}

For the symbolic DNN expansion rules for
e
, the symbolic DNN has to be recursively expanded for each expression within
e
. This can be seen in G-SOLVER. The rest of the rules presented here are rules that require more than only recursively applying the DNN expansion procedure. For
map
, the
\mathsf{expand}
function has to be called on the input expression to
map
. Below, the function
applyFunc
is used because the symbolic semantics can output a conditional value. Since
\sigma
only contains expanded values, for any function call expression,
\mathsf{expand}
must be called on each argument expression. In
traverse
, the DNN has to be expanded to add new neurons because the output of
traverse
is a polyhedral expression with fresh variables.

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\\
\frac{\mu=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}n^{\prime}_{i}*\mu_{b_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptF(f_{c})=(x_{1},x_{2}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j]\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma_{i}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{b_{i}},x_{2}\mapsto n^{\prime}_{i}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s},F,\sigma_{i},\mathcal{D}_{S_{i-1}},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}}{\texttt{applyFunc}(\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}},\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{P},f_{c},\mu)\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{j}},\mathcal{C}_{j}}\quad\textsc{lab = {G-map-poly}}\hskip 5.69046pt\frac{\mu=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}*\mu_{b_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptF(f_{c})=(x_{1},x_{2}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j]\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma_{i}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{b_{i}},x_{2}\mapsto\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s},F,\sigma_{i},\mathcal{D}_{S_{i-1}},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}}{\texttt{applyFunc}(\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}},\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{P},f_{c},\mu)\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{j}},\mathcal{C}_{j}}\quad\textsc{lab = {G-map-sym}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall 8.19447pt\mathsf{expand}(e_{i},\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\overline{\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}}},\overline{\mathcal{C}_{i}})=\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{1},\cdots x_{n}\mapsto\mu_{n}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s},F,\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{n},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models f_{c}(e_{1},\cdots e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}},\mathcal{C}_{0}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\_\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)=\mathsf{true}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip \\
\frac{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e_{1}\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}\models e_{2}\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{solver}}}(\mathsf{op},e_{1},e_{2})\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {G-solver}}\hskip 5.69046pt\frac{\mu=If(\mu^{\prime},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{applyFunc}(\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}},\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{P},f_{c},\mu_{1})=\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\texttt{applyFunc}(\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{P},f_{c},\mu_{2})=\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\texttt{applyFunc}(\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}},\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{P},f_{c},\mu)\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab = {G-map-r}}\\

\frac{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{0}},\mathcal{C}_{0}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{N}=[n^{\prime}_{1},\cdots n^{\prime}_{j}]\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j],\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathsf{add}(n^{\prime}_{i},\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S_{i-1}},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}_{i-1})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}_{S_{j}}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{C}_{j}\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mu_{b_{0}}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}\mu_{b_{i}}*n^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j],\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{i-1}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{i-1},\mathcal{P}\models f_{c_{2}}(n^{\prime}_{i},\mu_{b_{i}})\leadsto\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{j}}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j],\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{S_{i-1}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i-1},\mathcal{P}\models f_{c_{3}}(n^{\prime}_{i},\mu_{b_{i}})\leadsto\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models x\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e\}\leadsto\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{S_{j}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{j}}\quad\textsc{lab = {G-traverse}}\end{array}

G.2.
Symbolic Semantics for Expressions in
ConstraintFlow

The general form for symbolic semantics is
\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. In the same way as operational semantics,
F
contains mappings of function names to their arguments and return expressions.
\sigma
contains mappings from variables to expanded symbolic values.
\mathcal{D}_{S}
contains mappings from the shape members and metadata for each neuron in the symbolic DNN to symbolic values.
\mathcal{C}
is a conjunction of the constraints associated with each neuron’s abstract shape and any constraints on fresh variables that have been generated by the symbolic semantics. The output of symbolic semantics is a symbolic value and a conjunction of
\mathcal{C}
and any new constraints introduced for the symbolic variables in
\mu
. The symbolic semantics for
traverse
and
solver
are where additional constraints are introduced. In
traverse
, the symbolic semantics verifies the user-defined inductive invariant and then outputs a polyhedral expression with fresh variables and adds the constraint that this polyhedral expression satisfies the inductive invariant. For
\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{solver}}}(\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{minimize}}},e_{1},e_{2})
, the symbolic semantics outputs a fresh real valued symbolic variable,
\mu
, and adds the constraint that when
e_{2}
is true,
\mu\leq e_{1}
.

\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\langle c,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow c,\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-const}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{}{\langle x,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\sigma(x),\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-var}}\\
\\
\frac{}{\langle{\epsilon},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow{\epsilon}_{new},\mathcal{C}\wedge(-1\leq\epsilon_{new}\leq 1)}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-noise}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\langle e_{1}\oplus e_{2},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-binary}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{b_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{3},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{2}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{3},\mathcal{C}_{3}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu=If(\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})}{\langle(e_{1}?e_{2}:e_{3}),F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}_{3}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-ternary}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow n,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu=\mathcal{D}_{S}[n[m]]}{\langle e[m],F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-metadata}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e.F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow n,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu=\mathcal{D}_{S}[n[x]]}{\langle e[x],F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-shape}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(e),F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{max}}}(\mu),\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-max}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(\mu,f_{c},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}compare}}(e,f_{c}),F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-compare}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[n],\ \langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptF(f_{c})=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}),e\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x_{1}\mapsto\mu_{1},\cdots x_{n}\mapsto\mu_{n}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{n}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}{\langle f_{c}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}),F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-func-call}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(\mu_{b}^{\prime},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}{\langle e.\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(f_{c}),F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{b},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-map}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathcal{N}=[n^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,n^{\prime}_{j}]\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mu_{r_{0}}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}\mu_{r_{i}}*n^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x\mapsto\mu_{b}]\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}_{0}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j],\hskip 8.19447pt\langle f_{c_{2}}(n_{i},\mu_{r_{i}}),F,\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{C}_{j}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[j],\langle f_{c_{3}}(n_{i},\mu_{r_{i}}),F,\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu^{\prime\prime}=\mu_{r_{0}}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}If(\mu^{\prime}_{i},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{i},\mu_{r_{i}}*n_{i})\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma[x\mapsto\mu^{\prime\prime}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{j}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{unsat}(\neg(\mathcal{C}_{0}\wedge\mu_{b}^{\prime}\implies\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j}\wedge\mu^{\prime\prime\prime}))}{\mathsf{Ind}(x\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e\},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{b}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Chech-induction}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mathsf{unsat}(\neg(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\implies\mu))\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}^{\prime}=\mathsf{Ind}(x\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e\},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})}{\mathsf{Inv}(x\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e\},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mu_{b}\wedge\mu_{b}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Chech-invariant}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathsf{Inv}(x\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e\},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{true},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mu_{0}+\sum^{j}_{i=1}\mu_{i}*\mu^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x\mapsto\mu_{b}]\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\langle x\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e\},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{b},\mu\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-traverse}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{sat}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\wedge\mu_{2})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mu_{r}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{2}\wedge(\mu_{2}\implies\mu_{b}\leq\mu_{1})}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{solver}}}(\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{minimize}}},e_{1},e_{2}),F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{b},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-solver-min}}\\
\\
\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{1}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{sat}(\mathcal{C}_{2}\wedge\mu_{2})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{b}=\mu_{r}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{2}\wedge(\mu_{2}\implies\mu_{b}\geq\mu_{1})}{\langle\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{solver}}}(\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{maximize}}},e_{1},e_{2}),F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{b},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab = {Sym-solver-max}}\end{array}

Appendix H
Definitions for Over-Approximation

\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}_{S}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptX=\mathsf{Constants}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.19447ptY=\mathsf{Neurons}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymbolicVars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptZ=\mathsf{PolyExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{Constraints}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\exists X\forall Y\exists Z(\mathcal{C}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))}{\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}_{S}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Over-approx-dnn}}}\end{array}

\nu,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\mu,\mathcal{D}_{S}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathcal{C}=(\nu_{b}=\mu_{b})}{CS(\nu_{b},\mu_{b})=\mathcal{C}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Base-val-constraints}}}\\
\\
\frac{\nu_{l}=[\nu_{b_{1}},\cdots,\nu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l}=[\mu_{b_{1}},\cdots,\mu_{b_{n}}]}{CS(\nu_{l},\mu_{l})=\bigwedge^{n}_{i=1}(\mu_{b_{i}}=\nu_{b_{i}})}\quad\textsc{lab={{List-val-constraints-b}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\nu_{l}=[\nu_{b_{1}},\cdots,\nu_{b_{n}}]\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{l}=If(\mu_{b_{1}},\mu_{l_{1}},\mu_{l_{2}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}_{1}=CS(\nu_{l},\mu_{l_{1}})\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{C}_{2}=CS(\nu_{l},\mu_{l_{2}})}{CS(\nu_{l},\mu_{l})=(\mu_{b_{1}}\implies\mathcal{C}_{1})\wedge(\neg(\mu_{b_{1}})\implies\mathcal{C}_{2})}\quad\textsc{lab={{List-val-constraints-r}}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptX=\mathsf{Constants}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.19447ptY=\mathsf{Neurons}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymbolicVars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptZ=\mathsf{PolyExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\exists X\forall Y\exists Z(CS(\nu,\mu)\wedge\mathcal{C}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))}{\nu,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\mu,\mathcal{D}_{S}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Over-approx-val}}}\end{array}

[\nu],\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}[\mu],\mathcal{D}_{S}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\nu\text{ and }\mu\text{ have the same length}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptX=\mathsf{Constants}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.19447ptY=\mathsf{Neurons}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymbolicVars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptZ=\mathsf{PolyExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\exists X\forall Y\exists Z(\bigwedge_{i}CS(\nu_{i},\mu_{i})\wedge\mathcal{C}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))}{[\nu],\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}[\mu],\mathcal{D}_{S}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Over-approx-val-list}}}\end{array}

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{dom}(\rho)=\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptX=\mathsf{Constants}(\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C})\hskip 8.19447ptY=\mathsf{Neurons}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymbolicVars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptZ=\mathsf{PolyExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\cup\mathsf{SymExps}(\mathcal{D}_{S})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\exists X\forall Y\exists Z(\mathcal{C}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)}CS(\rho(t),\rho^{\prime}(t)))}{\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\rho^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{S}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Over-approx-store}}}\end{array}

\rho
represents the concrete store, which is a mapping from variables to concrete values.
\sigma
represents the symbolic store, which is a mapping from variables to symbolic values. In our symbolic semantics, we will only add expanded values to
\sigma
. Formally,
\forall t\in\sigma,\mathsf{expanded}(\sigma(t))

F\sim\Gamma,\tau_{s}

\Theta\sim\Gamma,\tau_{s}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathsf{dom}(F)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall f\in F,\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash F(f)\ :\ \Gamma(f)}{F\sim\Gamma,\tau_{s}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Sim-f}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall\theta\in\Theta,\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash\Theta(\theta)\ :\ \Gamma(\theta)}{\Theta\sim\Gamma,\tau_{s}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Sim-$\Theta$}}}\end{array}

\rho\sim\Gamma

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathsf{dom}(\rho)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall x\in\rho,\ \cdot\vdash\rho(x)\ :\ \Gamma(x)}{\rho\sim\Gamma}\quad\textsc{lab={{Sim-store}}}\end{array}

\sigma\sim\Gamma

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall x\in\sigma,\ \vdash\sigma(x)\ :\ \mathcal{R}(\Gamma(x))}{\sigma\sim\Gamma}\quad\textsc{lab={{Sim-sym-store}}}\end{array}

\mathcal{D}_{C}\sim\tau_{s}

\mathcal{D}_{S}\sim\tau_{s}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\forall n\in\mathcal{D}_{C}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall x\in\tau_{s},\ \vdash\mathcal{D}_{C}(n[x])\ :\ \tau_{s}(x)}{\mathcal{D}_{C}\sim\tau_{s}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Sim-dnn}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\forall n\in\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall x\in\tau_{s},\ \vdash\mathcal{D}_{S}(n[x])\ :\ \mathcal{R}(\tau_{s}(x))}{\mathcal{D}_{S}\sim\tau_{s}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Sim-sym-dnn}}}\end{array}

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\mathsf{expand}(e,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Multistep-expand}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Multistep-step}}}\\
\\
\frac{\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Multistep-add}}}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Multistep-r}}}\end{array}

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(CS(\mu,\nu)\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\wedge\mathcal{M}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))}{\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab={{Bisimulation}}}\end{array}

Appendix I
lemmas

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\exists X\forall Y\exists Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

then,

(1)

\exists X\forall Y\exists!Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

Proof sketch.

This is a stronger claim than what is needed to prove over-approximation. We can make this claim because given a DNN during concrete execution, there will be concrete polyhedral and symbolic expressions representing the shape members. For example,
\mathcal{D}_{C}(n[L])
might be
4n_{5}+2n_{3}+1
. In this case,
\mathcal{D}_{S}(n[L])=\mu_{1}
, where
\mu_{1}\in Z
. For each assignment of
n_{5}
and
n_{3}
,
4n_{5}+2n_{3}+1
equals a specific real number. Similarly, for all elements of
\mathcal{D}_{C}
,
t
, given an assignment to
Y
,
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)
is a real number. Since
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)
has to equal the same real number. When we create
\mathcal{D}_{S}
, we have a single symbolic variable representing each element
\mathsf{range}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
. During graph expansion, no variables are added to
Z
, so every variable of
Z
is in
\mathsf{range}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

X\subseteq\mathsf{Constants}(\mathcal{D}_{S})

(2)

\exists X\forall Y\exists Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

then,

(1)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

Proof sketch.

This is a stronger claim than what is needed to prove overapproximation. This lemma is only true when
X
only contains variables from
\mathcal{D}_{S}
. There are two possibilities for each variable in
X
, either it is in the range of
\mathcal{D}_{S}
or it is not. First, we could have a situation where
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=x_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=5
. In this case, there is a unique assignment to
x_{1}
so that
x_{1}=5
. Second, we could have a situation where
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=x_{1}n_{1}+x_{2}n_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=2n_{1}+2n_{2}
. Since we have to satisfy the condition that
\exists x_{1}\forall n_{1},n_{2}(x_{1}n_{1}+x_{2}n_{2}=2n_{1}+2n_{2})
, there is a unique assignment to
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. If the
\exists X
quantifier were to the right of the
\forall Y
quantifier, this lemma would not be true.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

X\subseteq\mathsf{Constants}(\mathcal{D}_{S})

(2)

\exists X\forall Y\exists Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

then,

(1)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

Proof sketch.

This lemma combines the previous two lemmas to form a stronger claim about how the symbolic graph over-approximates the concrete graph.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

then,

(1)

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that the output conditions from symbolic execution are always a conjunction of the input conditions and another set of conditions. This structure comes from the fact that in the rules for symbolic execution semantics, there are two rules that create new conditions and return a conjunction of them with the input
\mathcal{C}
, and the rest of the rules, just recursively accumulate the conditions outputted by symbolically executing the sub-expressions. The idea of these conditions is to add a condition whenever a fresh variable is generated for
traverse
or
solver
calls.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\forall i\in[n],\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e_{i},F,\rho_{i},\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu_{i},\mu_{i},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{i}

(2)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(\mathcal{C}_{0}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

then,

(1)

\forall i\in[n],\langle e_{i},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that if expressions,
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}
can all be symbolically evaluated under the conditions
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, then they can be symbolically evaluated sequentially, where
e_{i}
is evaluated under the conditions outputted by
e_{i-1}
. For most of the symbolic evaluation rules, the input conditions are simply propagated to the output conditions, so changing the input conditions will not affect whether or not the rules can be applied. In two rules,
Sym-traverse
and
Sym-solver
, the input conditions are used. For
Sym-traverse
, we have to prove that when symbolically evaluating
e_{i}
, if
\mathsf{Inv}
was true when called with input condition
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, then it will still be true when called with input condition
\mathcal{C}_{i-1}
, which is the output condition of symbolically evaluating
e_{i-1}
. For
Sym-solver
, we also have to prove that if the conjunction of the input constraint and the condition outputted by the symbolic evaluation of the input constraint were satisfiable before, then that conjunction is still satisfiable when starting with the condition
\mathcal{C}_{i-1}
instead of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. These are proved using Lemma
I.4
.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\forall i\in[n],\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e_{i},F,\rho_{i},\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{0}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu_{i},\mu_{i},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{i}

(2)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(\mathcal{C}_{0}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

then,

(1)

\forall i\in[n],\langle e_{i},F,\sigma_{i},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}_{i-1}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i}

(2)

\exists!X^{\prime}\forall Y^{\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime}(\bigwedge_{i}CS(\mu_{i},\nu_{i})\wedge\mathcal{C}_{n}\wedge\mathcal{M}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(3)

\mathcal{C}_{n}\implies\mathcal{C}_{0}

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that if expressions,
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}
can all be symbolically evaluated under the conditions
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, and the bisumlation holds, then they can all be symbolically evaluated sequentially, where
e_{i}
is evaluated under the conditions outputted by
e_{i-1}
, and the bisimulation holds on all the output symbolic values simultaneously, meaning there exists a unique assignment to
X
, for all assignments to
Y
, there exists a unique assignment to
Z
such that all of the symbolic values equal the concrete values, each element of the range of
\mathcal{D}_{S}
equals the corresponding element in the range of
\mathcal{D}_{C}
, the conditions outputted by symbolically evaluating
e_{n}
holds, and some condition
M
holds. In this proof,
M
is set to a conjunction of each
M_{i}
from the original bisumulation of each
e_{i}
. This proof uses Lemma
I.5
to show that the expressions can be symbolically evaluated sequentially. Then, it uses the fact that the condition outputted from the symbolic evaluation of each expression only adds constraints on fresh variables that are not present in the symbolic evaluation of other expressions. The second antecedent to this lemma implies that the assignment to the shared variables in the symbolic evaluation of
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}
have the same assignments in their individual bisumulation. Because of this, the assignment to
X^{\prime}
in the third consequent can be created by combining the assignments to each
X_{i}
used in each original bisimulation argument.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}

(2)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{1}

(3)

\mathsf{expand}(e,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

(4)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

then,

(1)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{2}

(2)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime})

(3)

\exists!X^{\prime}\forall Y^{\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime}(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S})}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(4)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that if bisimulation holds for an expression
e
with a symbolic graph
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
and then the symbolic graph is expanded to
\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
using the
\mathsf{expand}
function with input expression
e
, then, bisimulation holds for
e
with symbolic graph
\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and the output of symbolically evaluating
e
using
\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
will be in expanded form. We use induction on the structure of
e
to prove this. Showing that the new symbolic output will always be in expanded form is straightforward for most types of expressions because for most expressions, if the sub-expressions evaluate to expanded symbolic values, then the whole expression will also. Two exceptions to this are the expressions
e[x]
, where
x
is a shape member, and
e[m]
, where
m
is a type of metadata. Since
\mathsf{expand}
expands all shape members and metadata used in
e
, the symbolic values of accessing shape members will always be expanded.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu,\hat{\mathcal{C}_{1}},\mathcal{M}_{1}

(2)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{S}(t))

(3)

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

(4)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}

then,

(1)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}

(2)

\exists!X^{\prime}\forall Y^{\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime}(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t))

(3)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime})

(4)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that if bisimulation holds for an expression
e
with a symbolic graph
\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}
and then the symbolic graph is expanded to
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
using the mutli-step expand rules (for
\leadsto^{*}
), then bisimulation holds for
e
with symbolic graph
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and if the output of symbolically evaluating
e
using
\mathcal{D}_{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
is in expanded form, then the output of symbolically evaluating
e
using
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is in expanded form. The proof for this lemma uses structural induction on the rules defining
\leadsto^{*}
, which is comprised of arbitrary combinations of the following functions,
\mathsf{expand}
,
\mathsf{add}
, and
\leadsto
. It uses the Lemma
I.7
to handle the case of
\mathsf{expand}
and the rules for
\mathsf{add}
for the case of
\mathsf{add}
. For the case of
\leadsto
, this proof uses induction on the structure of
e
. The basic argument in all of these cases is that the original bisimulation arguments holds only if the symbolic semantics rules outputted a value for
e
, which is only possible if the graph was expanded enough for all of the sub-expressions in
e
. If the graph expands for other expressions, the conditions in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
should ensure that the symbolic value outputted for
e
using
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is equivalent to the symbolic value outputted for
e
using
\mathcal{D}_{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}

(2)

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

(3)

\langle e,F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu

(4)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(5)

Inductive invariants are true and Solver constraints are feasible

then,

(1)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime}

(2)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{M}

(3)

\exists!X^{\prime}\forall Y^{\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime}(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S})}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that if a expression can be evaluated under concrete semantics and the symbolic graph has been created and expanded for
e
, then bisimulation is true for the concrete and symbolic values given by the operational and symbolic semantics. The antecedent of this lemma includes that all inductive invariants are true and solver constraints are feasible. The antecedent also includes the symbolic store over-approximating the concrete store and a stronger assumption on the symbolic graph over-approximating the concrete DNN because both of these statements are required to strengthen the induction hypothesis in order to prove this lemma. This lemma is proved by induction on the structure of
e
. It uses Lemmas
I.6

I.7

I.8
. We present the complicated cases in the induction on the structure of
e
. The other cases use similar arguments.
∎

Lemma I.0.

If

(1)

\Gamma,\tau_{s}\vdash e\ :\ t

(2)

\bot\sqsubset t\sqsubset\top

(3)

F\sim\Gamma,\tau_{s}

(4)

\rho\sim\Gamma

(5)

\mathcal{D}_{C}\sim\tau_{s}

(6)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S}

(7)

\exists!X\forall Y\exists!Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(8)

Inductive invariants are true and Solver constraints are feasible

then,

(1)

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

(2)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}

Proof sketch.

This lemma states that if an expression,
e
, type-checks, the inductive invariants are true and the solver input constraints are feasible, then given a symbolic graph,
\mathcal{D}_{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
, that over-approximates the concrete DNN,
\mathcal{D}_{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
can be expanded for
e
. This proof uses Lemma
I.9
because some of the graph expansion rules, such as the ones for
map
, require executing the symbolic semantics during graph expansion. We present the complicated cases in induction on the structure of
e
. The other cases use similar arguments.
∎

Theorem
5.3
.

For a well-typed program
\Pi
, if
ProveSound
verification procedure proves it maintains the property
\mathcal{P}
, then upon executing
\Pi
on all concrete DNNs within the bound of verification, the property
\mathcal{P}
will be maintained at all neurons in the DNN.

Theorem
5.4
.

If executing a well-typed program
\Pi
that does not use
traverse
and
solver
constructs on all concrete DNNs within the bounds of verification maintains the property
\mathcal{P}
for all neurons in the DNN, then it can be proved by the
ProveSound
verification procedure.

Appendix J
Proofs

Proof.

Proof of lemma
I.1

(1)

Let
m_{X}
be a satisfying assignment to
X
and
m_{Y}
be an assignment to
Y
.

(2)

For a given assignment to
Y
, for all
t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{C})
,
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)
is a fixed value.

(3)

m_{1},m_{2}
are two assignments of
Z
.

(a)

m_{X}\cup m_{Y}\cup m_{1}\models(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(b)

m_{X}\cup m_{Y}\cup m_{2}\models(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(4)

Z\subseteq\mathsf{range}(\mathcal{D}_{S})

(5)

For each
z
in
Z
, there is a
t
in
\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
s.t.
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=z

(6)

From (3a) and (5), for each
z
in
Z
,
m_{1}(z)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)

(7)

From (3b) and (5), for each
z
in
Z
,
m_{2}(z)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)

(8)

From (6) and (7), for each
z
in
Z
,
m_{1}(z)=m_{2}(z)

(9)

So, for given assignments
m_{X}
and
m_{Y}
, there is a unique assignment to
Z
.

(10)

Therefore,
\exists X\forall Y\exists!Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

∎

Proof.

Proof of lemma
I.2

(1)

m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are two satisfying assignments of
X

(2)

There are two types of variables in
X
:

(a)

For some of the
t
in
\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
,
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)
are polyhedral expressions or symbolic expressions. For such values, there are corresponding variables in
X
which represent the coefficients and the constants of the polyhedral expressions or the symbolic expressions.

(b)

For the remaining
t
in
\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
,
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)
is some constant
c
, an
x
in
X
, represents this constant, s.t.,
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=x
.

(3)

In case (2a),

(a)

Let
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=c_{0}+c_{1}*n_{1}+c_{2}*n_{2}+\cdots
.

In this case,
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=x_{0}+x_{1}*n_{1}+x_{2}*n_{2}+\cdots
where
n_{1},n_{2},\cdots\in Y
.

Since the equality
c_{0}+c_{1}*n_{1}+c_{2}*n_{2}+\cdots=x_{0}+x_{1}*n_{1}+x_{2}*n_{2}+\cdots
must hold for all assignments to
Y
, there is a unique assignment to
x_{0},x_{1},\cdots
.

(b)

Let
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=c_{0}+c_{1}*\epsilon_{1}+c_{2}*\epsilon_{2}+\cdots
.

In this case,
\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=x_{0}+x_{1}*\epsilon_{1}+x_{2}*\epsilon_{2}+\cdots
where
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\cdots\in Y

Since the equality
c_{0}+c_{1}*\epsilon_{1}+c_{2}*\epsilon_{2}+\cdots=x_{0}+x_{1}*\epsilon_{1}+x_{2}*\epsilon_{2}+\cdots
must hold for all assignments to
Y
, there is a unique assignment to
x_{0},x_{1},\cdots
.

(4)

In case (2b), since
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)
is a constant, there is a unique assignment to
x
such that
\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)
, i.e.,
x=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)

Hence, there is a unique assignment to all the variables in
X
.

(5)

Therefore,
\exists!X\forall Y\exists Z(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S})}\mathcal{D}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

∎

and lemma

Proof of lemma

.

This can be seen by looking at the rules in the section symbolic semantics for expressions. We can do induction on the structure of

to conclude that in each rule,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is either equal to
\mathcal{C}
, in which case
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{true}
or
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is created by taking conjunctions of
\mathcal{C}
with other conditions.
∎
