Proof of Lemma
I.8
.

\leadsto^{*}
is recursively defined as a sequence of one of the following three operations:

(1)

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

(2)

\mathsf{expand}(e,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

(3)

\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

We can prove this lemma using structural induction on
\leadsto^{*}
.

Base Case:
\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

There are two rules for
\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
:

(1)

ADD-NEURON-B

(a)

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\quad\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}

(b)

Consequents 1, 2 and 4 follow from (a) and Antecedents 1, 2 and 4.

(c)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)

(d)

Consequent 3 follows from (a) and (c)

(2)

Add-neuron-r

(a)

\frac{n\not\in\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\textsf{Metadata}[m_{1},\cdots,m_{k}]\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[k],\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{add}(n,m,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S_{i-1}})\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\textsf{Shape}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{l}]\hskip 8.19447pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{0}}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S_{k}}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\forall i\in[l],\mathcal{D}_{S_{i}}^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{add}(n,x_{i},\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{i-1}})}{\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{D}_{S_{l}}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}\wedge\mathcal{P}(n,\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{l}})}

(b)

Since the same symbolic variables are used for neurons in concrete operational semantics and symbolic operation semantics,
n\in\mathcal{D}_{C}
.

(c)

X^{\prime}
can be defined as the constants in
\mathcal{D}_{S}
and the constants in the metadata and shape elements of
n
that are added to form
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{S_{l}}

(d)

X^{\prime}\setminus X
consists of elements in the range of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}
. We can create an assignment
m_{x^{\prime}}
to the set
X^{\prime}\setminus X
. From (b), for each
x\in X^{\prime}\setminus X
, there exists
t
such that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=x
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{C}(t)=c
for some constant
c
. We can ass
[x\mapsto c]
to
m_{x^{\prime}}
.

(e)

We can call the unique assignment to
X
satisfying antecedent (2),
m_{x}
and create an assignment
m^{\prime}_{x}=m_{x}\cup m_{x^{\prime}}
. Using
m^{\prime}_{x}
, given an arbitrary assignment to
Y
, every expression in the range of
\mathcal{D}_{C}
will have a fixed, constant value.

(f)

Y^{\prime}=Y\cup Y^{\prime\prime}
, where
Y^{\prime\prime}
includes
n
and new neurons and symbolic variables used to define the shape and metadata of
n
.

(g)

Given an arbitrary assignment to
Y^{\prime}
, we can call the unique assignment to
Z
that satisfies Antecedent (2),
m_{z}
. We can construct
m_{z^{\prime}}
. From (b) and (e), for each
z\in Z^{\prime}\setminus Z
, there exists
t\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}
, such that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=z
. We can add
[z\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)]
. Call
m^{\prime}_{z}=m_{z}\cup m_{z^{\prime}}

(h)

From (d) and (g),
\exists X^{\prime}\forall Y^{\prime}\exists Z^{\prime}(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S})}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(i)

From lemma
I.3
using (h),
\exists X^{\prime}!\forall Y^{\prime}\exists Z^{\prime}!(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S})}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))
. This is Consequent (2).

(j)

For all
t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S}),\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=hh_{S}(t)
because ADD-NEURON-R does not change any mappings within
\mathcal{D}_{S}
.

(k)

\mathcal{P}(n,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S})
is only defined on the shape and metadata of
n
.

(l)

From (j) and (k),
\langle e,F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rangle\downarrow\mu,\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\quad\mu^{\prime}=\mu

(m)

Using (i), (l), Lemma
I.4
and Antecedent (1),
\exists X^{\prime}!\forall Y^{\prime}\exists Z^{\prime}!(CS(\mu,\nu)\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\wedge\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S})}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(n)

Consequent (1) follows from Antecedent (1), (l), and (m).

(o)

Consequent (2) follows from (l)

(p)

Consequent (4) follows from (i) and Antecedents (2) and (4).

Base Case:

\mathsf{expand}(e,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

Consequents (1),(2),(3) and (4) directly follow from Lemma
I.7
using Antecedents (1),(2),(3) and (4).

Base Case:

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e\leadsto\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

We will prove this by structural induction on
e
.

(1)

Base Cases:
e\equiv c
or
e\equiv x

(a)

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\quad\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}

(b)

Consequents 1, 2 and 4 follow from (a) and Antecedents 1, 2 and 4.

(c)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)

(d)

Consequent 3 follows from (a) and (c)

(2)

Induction Case:
e\equiv e_{1}\oplus e_{2}

(a)

\frac{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e_{1}\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{P}\models e_{2}\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e_{1}\oplus e_{2}\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}}
From Antecedent (3) and
G-binary

(b)

\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{2}}{\langle e_{1}\oplus e_{2},F,\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\rangle\Downarrow\nu_{1}\oplus\nu_{2}}
from Antecedent (1) and
Op-binary

(c)

From (a),
\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\models e_{1}\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}

(d)

From (a),
\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{P}\models e_{2}\leadsto\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}

(e)

\frac{\langle e_{1},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\langle e_{2},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{2}}{\langle e_{1}\oplus e_{2},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C}\rangle\downarrow\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}}
from Antecedent (1) and
Sym-binary

(f)

From the induction hypothesis on
e
using (b), (c) and Antecedents (1),(2) and (4):

(i)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e_{1},F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu_{1},\mu^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{1}

(ii)

\exists!X^{\prime}\forall Y^{\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime}(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}})}\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}}(t))

(iii)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{1})\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime}_{1})

(iv)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}}

(g)

From the induction hypothesis on
e
using (b), (d), (i), (ii) and (iv):

(i)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e_{2},F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu_{2},\mu^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}_{2},\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{2}

(ii)

\exists!X^{\prime\prime}\forall Y^{\prime\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime\prime}(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}})}\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}}(t))
This is Consequent (2).

(iii)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{2})\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime}_{2})

(iv)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}}
.
This is Consequent (4).

(h)

From the induction hypothesis on
e
using (d), (f i), (f ii) and (f iv):

(i)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e_{1},F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu_{1},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{1}

(ii)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime}_{1})\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1})

(i)

From Lemma
I.6
using (g i) and (h i):

(i)

\langle e_{1},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}\rangle\downarrow\mu^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}

(ii)

\exists!X^{\prime\prime\prime}\forall Y^{\prime\prime\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime\prime\prime}(CS(\nu_{1},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1})\wedge CS(\nu_{2},\mu^{\prime}_{2})\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}\wedge\mathcal{M}\wedge\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}})}\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{C}(t))

(j)

From (b), (h i), (i i), (i ii), and
Sym-binary
,
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu_{1}\oplus\nu_{2},\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1}\oplus\mu^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{M}
. This is Consequent (1).

(k)

From (f iii), (g iii), (h ii) and
Expanded-binary
,

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2})\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime\prime}_{1}\oplus\mu^{\prime}_{2})
. This is Consequent (3).

(3)

The other induction cases are similar.
G-traverse
involves
\mathsf{add}
so the induction case for
e\equiv x\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{traverse}}}(\delta,f_{c_{1}},f_{c_{2}},f_{c_{3}})\{e_{1}\}
will use the proof of the first base case above (
\mathsf{add}(n,\tau_{s},\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
).
G-map
and
G-func-call
involve
\mathsf{expand}
, so the proof of the induction cases
e\equiv e\cdot\texttt{\footnotesize{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.05,0.9}{map}}}(f_{c})
and
e\equiv f_{c}(e_{1},\cdots e_{n})
will use the proof of the second base case above (
\mathsf{expand}(e,\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{S},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
).

Induction Case:

\frac{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}}

(1)

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}

(2)

\tau_{s},F,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{P}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}

(3)

From the induction hypothesis. using (1) and Antecedents (1),(2) and (4):

(a)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}},\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{1}

(b)

\exists!X^{\prime}\forall Y^{\prime}\exists!Z^{\prime}(\bigwedge_{t\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}})}\mathcal{D}_{C}(t)=\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}}(t))

(c)

\mathsf{expanded}(\mu)\implies\mathsf{expanded}(\mu^{\prime})

(d)

\rho,\mathcal{D}_{C}\prec_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{S_{1}}

(4)

From the induction hypothesis using (2), (3 a), (3 b) and (3 d):

(a)

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}e,F,\rho,\sigma,\mathcal{D}_{C},\mathcal{D}_{S_{2}},\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\updownarrow\nu,\mu^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{2}

(b)

(5)

Consequent (3) follows from (3 c) and (4 c).

∎

.

Proof for theorem
5.3
.

From Lemma
I.10
, we can conclude that for each abstract transformer
\theta
specified in a
ConstraintFlow
program
\Pi
, we can create an expanded symbolic DNN that can over-approximate a given concrete DNN that is within the bounds of the verification procedure. From Lemma
I.9
, the verification procedure and the operational semantics execute the abstract transformer on the symbolic DNN and the concrete DNN respectively, to output a tuple of symbolic and concrete values respectively, representing the new abstract shape, with the symbolic values over-approximating the concrete values. Hence if the soundness property holds on the over-approximated symbolic output, i.e., the abstract transformer is sound on the symbolic DNN, then the transformer is also sound on the concrete DNN.
∎

Proof for theorem
5.4
.

For all of the constructs except for
solver
and
traverse
, the symbolic semantics are exact w.r.t. the operational semantics. So, we can prove, using structural induction, that given the output of symbolic evaluation of an expression,
e
,
\mu,\mathcal{C}
, defined on
\mathsf{vars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
, for every assignment to
\mathsf{vars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
, that satisfies
\mathcal{C}
, there exists a concrete DNN,
\mathcal{D}_{C}
, s.t. concretely evaluating
e
using the operational semantics will output
v
equal to the value of
\mu
under the given assignment to
\mathsf{vars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
. If the
ProveSound
verification procedure fails, it will return a satisfying assignment to
\mathsf{vars}(\mathcal{D}_{S})
, which can be mapped to a concrete DNN that does not satisfy the over-approximation based soundness property.
∎

Appendix K
DNN Certifiers used for Evaluation

In this section, we provide the details and the
ConstraintFlow
codes for the DNN certifiers and the transformers used in the evaluation in §
6
.

K.1.
Dataset for Evaluation of Unsound Transformers

To create the dataset for evaluating the detection of unsound behavior in the DNN certifiers, we randomly introduced bugs in the existing as well as the new certifiers defined in the evaluation. The following bugs were injected to create unsound behavior:

(1)

Changing the operations to other operations with similar types of operands. For instance, changing + to -, max to min, etc.

(2)

Changing the shape member to another shape member with the same type. For instance, changing
curr
[l] to
curr
[u].

(3)

Changing function calls to other functions with the same signature.

(4)

Changing the neurons, for example,
prev
to
curr
, when
prev
represents a single neuron.

K.2.
ConstraintFlow
codes for Sigmoid and Tanh

In the following code, we give the specifications for Sigmoid and Tanh transformers. Similar specifications can be given for transformers corresponding to Exponential and Reciprocal DNN operations. An attention layer is a composition of these primitive operations.
The abstract transformers corresponding to all of these primitive operations can be specified in
ConstraintFlow
. However, only the transformers for which the verification queries can fit into a decidable SMT theory can be verified by the
ProveSound
verification procedure. In the following, we present the transformers for Sigmoid and Tanh operations for the DeepPoly certifier. These transformers for other DNN certifiers are quite similar and are thus, omitted here.

K.3.2.

Following is the code for the Vegas certifier. Vegas uses a forward analysis followed by a backward analysis, both using different transformers. The metadata
equations
is used to refer to a list of equations relating the neurons in the current layer to the neurons in
prev
. For the
rev_Affine
operation, the code uses the
solver
construct to find the minimum and maximum value of the neuron given the bounds of the neurons in
prev
.
